The **Supreme Court of the United States** (**SCOTUS**) is the [highest court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highest_court) in the [federal judiciary of the United States](1636627.md). It has ultimate [appellate jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appellate_jurisdiction) over all [U.S. federal court](2103888.md) cases, and over [state court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_court_\(United_States\)) cases that turn on questions of [U.S. constitutional](31644.md) or [federal law](27552742.md). It also has [original jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Original_jurisdiction_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States) over a narrow range of cases, specifically "all Cases affecting Ambassadors, other public Ministers and Consuls, and those in which a State shall be Party".[^cite_note-2] In [1803](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marbury_v._Madison), the court asserted the power of [judicial review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_review_in_the_United_States), the ability to invalidate a [statute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute) for violating a provision of the Constitution. It is also able to strike down [presidential directives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_directive) for violating either the Constitution or [statutory law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statutory_law).[^cite_note-3]

Under [Article Three of the United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_Three_of_the_United_States_Constitution), the composition and procedures of the Supreme Court were originally established by the [1st Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_United_States_Congress) through the [Judiciary Act of 1789](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary_Act_of_1789). As currently constituted[^cite_note-4] the court consists of nine justices—the [chief justice of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_justice_of_the_United_States) and eight [associate justices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Associate_Justice_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States)—who meet at the [Supreme Court Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Supreme_Court_Building) in [Washington, D.C.](108956.md) Justices have [lifetime tenure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lifetime_tenure), meaning they remain on the court until they die, retire, resign, or are [impeached](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_impeachment_in_the_United_States) and removed from office.[^cite_note-\:0-5] When a vacancy occurs, the [president](24113.md), with the [advice and consent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomination_and_confirmation_to_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States) of the [Senate](24909346.md), appoints a new justice.[^cite_note-uscourts.gov-6]

Each justice has a single vote in deciding the cases argued before the court. When in the majority, the chief justice decides who writes the [opinion of the court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_of_the_court); otherwise, the most senior justice in the majority assigns the task.[^cite_note-uscourts.gov-6] A justice may write an opinion in [concurrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concurring_opinion) with the court, or they may write a [dissent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissenting_opinion), and these concurrences or dissents may also be joined by other justices. On average, the Supreme Court receives about 7,000 petitions for [writs of certiorari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writs_of_certiorari) each year, but only grants about 80.[^cite_note-7]

## History

Main article: [History of the Supreme Court of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States)



The [Royal Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Exchange_\(New_York_City\)) in [New York City](645042.md), the first meeting place of the Supreme Court

Image of two-story brick building.

The court lacked its own building until 1935. For most of that time, it had chambers in the [Capitol Building](31979.md) in [Washington, D.C.](108956.md) Prior to that, between 1791 and 1801, the court met in [City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_City_Hall_\(Philadelphia\)) (pictured) in [Philadelphia](50585.md).

In 1787, four years after the end of the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War), delegates to the [1787 Constitutional Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Convention_\(United_States\)) convened in [Philadelphia](50585.md), where they debated the [separation of powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_powers) between the legislative and executive departments and established parameters for a [national judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_branch)[^cite_note-8] as a third branch of the [federal government](195149.md). In the [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain) tradition, judicial matters had been the responsibility of the [royal (executive) authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_\(government\)). During the Constitutional Convention, delegates opposed to having a [strong central government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_government) argued that national laws could be enforced by state courts. [James Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Madison) and others advocated for a national judicial authority chosen by the national legislature. It was proposed that the judiciary should have a role in checking executive branch power to [veto](74661.md) or revise laws.[^cite_note-9][^cite_note-10]

The [framers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Framers_of_the_Constitution) ultimately compromised by sketching only a general outline of the judiciary in [Article Three of the United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_Three_of_the_United_States_Constitution), vesting federal judicial power in "one supreme Court, and in such inferior Courts as the Congress may from time to time ordain and establish."[^cite_note-11] They did not delineate the exact powers or prerogatives of the Supreme Court or determine how the judicial branch should be organized.[^cite_note-12]

The 1st United States Congress provided the detailed organization of a federal [judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary) through the [Judiciary Act of 1789](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary_Act_of_1789). They decided that the Supreme Court, as the country's highest judicial tribunal, would be based in the [nation's capital](181337.md) and would be composed of a chief justice and five associate justices. The act also divided the country into judicial districts, which were in turn organized into circuits. Justices were required to hold [circuit court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circuit_court) twice a year in their assigned judicial district.[^cite_note-SCinstitution-13]

Immediately after signing the act into law, President [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington) nominated [John Jay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Jay) as the court's new chief justice, and [John Rutledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Rutledge), [William Cushing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Cushing), [Robert H. Harrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_H._Harrison), [James Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Wilson_\(Founding_Father\)) and [John Blair Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Blair_Jr.) as its associate justices. All six were confirmed by the [U.S. Senate](24909346.md) on September 26, 1789. Harrison declined to serve, and Washington later nominated [James Iredell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Iredell) to replace him.[^cite_note-SCnoms-14][^cite_note-15]

The Supreme Court held its inaugural session from February 2 through February 10, 1790, at the [Royal Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Exchange_\(New_York_City\)) in New York City, then the U.S. capital.[^cite_note-16] A second session was held there in August 1790.[^cite_note-17] The earliest sessions of the court were devoted to organizational proceedings, as the first cases did not reach it until 1791.[^cite_note-SCinstitution-13] When the nation's capital was moved to [Philadelphia](50585.md) in 1790, the Supreme Court moved to Philadelphia with it. After initially meeting in present-day [Independence Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Hall), the court established its [chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barrister's_chambers) at city hall. When the capital moved to Washington, D.C., the court was held in the [U.S. Capitol Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Capitol_Building) (see, e.g. [Old Supreme Court Chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Supreme_Court_Chamber)) until 1935 when it moved to its own building.[^cite_note-SCbuilding-18]

### Early beginnings

Further information: [Jay Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Court), [Rutledge Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutledge_Court), [Ellsworth Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellsworth_Court), and [Marshall Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Court)



[John Marshall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Marshall), chief justice from 1801 to 1835

Under chief justices Jay, Rutledge, and [Ellsworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Ellsworth) (1789–1801), the court heard few cases; its first decision was *[West v. Barnes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_v._Barnes)* (1791), a case involving procedure.[^cite_note-19] As the court initially had only six members, every decision that it made by a majority was also made by two-thirds (voting four to two).[^cite_note-20] However, Congress has always allowed less than the court's full membership to make decisions, starting with a [quorum](237014.md) of four justices in 1789.[^cite_note-21] The court lacked a home of its own and had little prestige,[^cite_note-tws31oct01-22] a situation not helped by the era's highest-profile case, *[Chisholm v. Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chisholm_v._Georgia)* (1793), which was reversed within two years by the adoption of the [Eleventh Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleventh_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution).[^cite_note-23]

The court's power and prestige grew substantially during the [Marshall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Marshall) Court (1801–1835).[^cite_note-tws31oct02-24] Under Marshall, the court established the power of [judicial review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_review_in_the_United_States) over acts of Congress,[^cite_note-25] including specifying itself as the supreme expositor of the [Constitution](31644.md) (*[Marbury v. Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marbury_v._Madison)*)[^cite_note-tws31oct05-26][^cite_note-tws31oct09-27] and making several important constitutional rulings that gave shape and substance to the [balance of power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balance_of_power_\(federalism\)) between the federal government and states, notably *[Martin v. Hunter's Lessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_v._Hunter's_Lessee)*, *[McCulloch v. Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McCulloch_v._Maryland)*, and *[Gibbons v. Ogden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gibbons_v._Ogden)*.[^cite_note-tws31oct03-28][^cite_note-tws31oct08-29][^cite_note-tws31oct04-30][^cite_note-tws31oct06-31]

The Marshall Court also ended the practice of each justice issuing his opinion *[seriatim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seriatim)*,[^cite_note-tws31oct11-32] a remnant of British tradition,[^cite_note-tws31oct10-33] and instead issuing a single majority opinion.[^cite_note-tws31oct11-32] Also during Marshall's tenure, although beyond the court's control, the impeachment and [acquittal](300834.md) of Justice [Samuel Chase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Chase) from 1804 to 1805 helped cement the principle of [judicial independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_independence).[^cite_note-tws31oct12-34][^cite_note-tws31oct13-35]

### From Taney to Taft

Further information: [Taney Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taney_Court), [Chase Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chase_Court), [Waite Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waite_Court), [Fuller Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuller_Court), [White Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Court_\(judges\)), and [Taft Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taft_Court)

The [Taney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_B._Taney) Court (1836–1864) made several important rulings, such as *[Sheldon v. Sill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheldon_v._Sill)*, which held that while Congress may not limit the subjects the Supreme Court may hear, it may limit the jurisdiction of the lower federal courts to prevent them from hearing cases dealing with certain subjects.[^cite_note-tws31oct16-36] Nevertheless, it is primarily remembered for its ruling in *[Dred Scott v. Sandford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dred_Scott_v._Sandford)*,[^cite_note-tws31oct21-37] which helped precipitate the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War).[^cite_note-tws4494-38] In the [Reconstruction era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_era), the [Chase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salmon_P._Chase), [Waite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morrison_Waite), and [Fuller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melville_Fuller) Courts (1864–1910) interpreted the new Civil War amendments to the Constitution[^cite_note-tws31oct06-31] and developed the doctrine of [substantive due process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Substantive_due_process) (*[Lochner v. New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lochner_v._New_York)*;[^cite_note-tws31oct24-39] *[Adair v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adair_v._United_States)*).[^cite_note-tws31oct100-40] The size of the court was last changed in 1869, when it was set at nine.

Under the [White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Douglass_White) and [Taft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Howard_Taft) Courts (1910–1930), the court held that the [Fourteenth Amendment](31666.md) had [incorporated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incorporation_\(Bill_of_Rights\)) some guarantees of the [Bill of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Bill_of_Rights) against the states (*[Gitlow v. New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gitlow_v._New_York)*),[^cite_note-twsff4frj-41] grappled with the new [antitrust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antitrust) statutes (*[Standard Oil Co. of New Jersey v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Oil_Co._of_New_Jersey_v._United_States)*), upheld the constitutionality of [military conscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_the_United_States) (*[Selective Draft Law Cases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selective_Draft_Law_Cases)*),[^cite_note-42] and brought the substantive due process doctrine to its first apogee (*[Adkins v. Children's Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adkins_v._Children's_Hospital)*).[^cite_note-tws31oct101-43]

### New Deal era

Further information: [Hughes Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hughes_Court), [Stone Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_Court_\(judges\)), [Vinson Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinson_Court), and [Warren Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren_Court)



The [U.S. Supreme Court Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Supreme_Court_Building) in Washington, D.C., the home of the Supreme Court since 1935

The Court seated

The [Hughes Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hughes_Court) in 1932, photographed by [Erich Salomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erich_Salomon). Members include Chief Justice [Charles Evans Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Evans_Hughes) (center), [Louis Brandeis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Brandeis), [Benjamin N. Cardozo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_N._Cardozo), [Harlan Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlan_Stone), [Owen Roberts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owen_Roberts), and the "[Four Horsemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Horsemen_\(Supreme_Court\))": [Pierce Butler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierce_Butler_\(justice\)), [James Clark McReynolds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Clark_McReynolds), [George Sutherland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Sutherland), and [Willis Van Devanter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willis_Van_Devanter), who opposed [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal) policies.

During the [Hughes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Evans_Hughes), [Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlan_Fiske_Stone), and [Vinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fred_M._Vinson) courts (1930–1953), the court gained its own accommodation in 1935[^cite_note-tws31oct-44] and [changed its interpretation of the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_switch_in_time_that_saved_nine), giving a broader reading to the powers of the federal government to facilitate President [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt)'s [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal) (most prominently *[West Coast Hotel Co. v. Parrish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_Hotel_Co._v._Parrish), [Wickard v. Filburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wickard_v._Filburn)*, *[United States v. Darby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Darby)*, and *[United States v. Butler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Butler)*).[^cite_note-tws31oct103-45][^cite_note-tws31oct107-46][^cite_note-tws31oct109-47] During [World War II](32927.md), the court continued to favor government power, upholding the [internment of Japanese Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internment_of_Japanese_Americans) (*[Korematsu v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korematsu_v._United_States)*) and the mandatory [Pledge of Allegiance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pledge_of_Allegiance) (*[Minersville School District v. Gobitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minersville_School_District_v._Gobitis)*). Nevertheless, *Gobitis* was soon repudiated (*[West Virginia State Board of Education v. Barnette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Virginia_State_Board_of_Education_v._Barnette)*), and the *[Steel Seizure Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youngstown_Sheet_&_Tube_Co._v._Sawyer)* restricted the pro-government trend.

The Warren Court (1953–1969) dramatically expanded the force of Constitutional [civil liberties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_liberties).[^cite_note-tws31oct110-48] It held that [segregation in public schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States) violates the [Equal Protection Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Protection_Clause) of the [Fourteenth Amendment](31666.md) (*[Brown v. Board of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_v._Board_of_Education)*, *[Bolling v. Sharpe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolling_v._Sharpe)*, and *[Green v. County School Bd.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_v._County_School_Board_of_New_Kent_County)*)[^cite_note-tws31oct113-49] and that [legislative districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_district) must be roughly equal in population (*[Reynolds v. Sims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reynolds_v._Sims)*). It recognized a general [right to privacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_privacy) (*[Griswold v. Connecticut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Griswold_v._Connecticut)*),[^cite_note-tws31oct114-50] limited the role of religion in public school, most prominently *[Engel v. Vitale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engel_v._Vitale)* and *[Abington School District v. Schempp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abington_School_District_v._Schempp)*,[^cite_note-tws31oct120-51][^cite_note-tws31oct121-52] [incorporated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incorporation_of_the_Bill_of_Rights) most guarantees of the Bill of Rights against the states, prominently *[Mapp v. Ohio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mapp_v._Ohio)* (the [exclusionary rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusionary_rule)) and *[Gideon v. Wainwright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gideon_v._Wainwright)* ([right to appointed counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_defender)),[^cite_note-tws31oct131-53][^cite_note-tws31oct203-54] and required that criminal suspects be apprised of all these rights by [police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police) (*[Miranda v. Arizona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miranda_v._Arizona)*).[^cite_note-tws31oct132-55] At the same time, the court limited [defamation](28661.md) suits by public figures (*[New York Times Co. v. Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times_Co._v._Sullivan)*) and supplied the government with an unbroken run of antitrust victories.[^cite_note-56]

### Burger, Rehnquist, and Roberts

Main articles: [Burger Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burger_Court), [Rehnquist Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rehnquist_Court), and [Roberts Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roberts_Court)



Supreme Court justices with President [George W. Bush](3414021.md) (center-right) in October 2005. Left to right are: [Ruth Bader Ginsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruth_Bader_Ginsburg), [David Souter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Souter), [Antonin Scalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonin_Scalia), [John Paul Stevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Paul_Stevens), [John Roberts](1928850.md), [Sandra Day O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandra_Day_O'Connor), [Anthony Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Kennedy), [Clarence Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Thomas), and [Stephen Breyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Breyer).

The Burger Court (1969–1986) saw a conservative shift.[^cite_note-57] It also expanded *Griswold*'s right to privacy to strike down [abortion laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_the_United_States) (*[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)*)[^cite_note-tws31oct204-58] but divided deeply on [affirmative action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action_in_the_United_States) (*[Regents of the University of California v. Bakke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regents_of_the_University_of_California_v._Bakke)*)[^cite_note-tws31oct205-59] and campaign finance regulation (*[Buckley v. Valeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buckley_v._Valeo)*).[^cite_note-tws31oct207-60] It also wavered on the [death penalty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_penalty_in_the_United_States), ruling first that most applications were defective (*[Furman v. Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Furman_v._Georgia)*),[^cite_note-tws31oct208-61] but later that the death penalty itself was not unconstitutional (*[Gregg v. Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregg_v._Georgia)*).[^cite_note-tws31oct208-61][^cite_note-history1-62][^cite_note-tws31oct2122-63]

The Rehnquist Court (1986–2005) was known for its revival of judicial enforcement of [federalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism),[^cite_note-tws31oct2brn2b-64] emphasizing the limits of the Constitution's affirmative grants of power (*[United States v. Lopez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Lopez)*) and the force of its restrictions on those powers (*[Seminole Tribe v. Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seminole_Tribe_v._Florida)*, *[City of Boerne v. Flores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Boerne_v._Flores)*).[^cite_note-tws31octrtr45-65][^cite_note-tws31oct309-66][^cite_note-tws31oct310-67][^cite_note-tws31oct4004-68][^cite_note-tws31oct555-69] It struck down single-sex state schools as a violation of equal protection (*[United States v. Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Virginia)*), laws against [sodomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodomy) as violations of substantive due process (*[Lawrence v. Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_v._Texas)*)[^cite_note-tws31oct34654-70] and the [line-item veto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line-item_veto) (*[Clinton v. New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_v._New_York)*) but upheld [school vouchers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_vouchers) (*[Zelman v. Simmons-Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zelman_v._Simmons-Harris)*) and [reaffirmed](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/reaffirm#English) *Roe*'s restrictions on abortion laws (*[Planned Parenthood v. Casey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_Parenthood_v._Casey)*).[^cite_note-tws31octffsfs-71] The court's decision in *[Bush v. Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_v._Gore)*, which ended the electoral recount during the [2000 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_presidential_election), remains especially controversial with debate ongoing over the rightful winner and whether or not the ruling should set a precedent.[^cite_note-rds21nov12-72][^cite_note-tws31oct44454-73][^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75]

The Roberts Court (2005–present) is regarded as more conservative and [controversial](#Criticism_and_controversies) than the Rehnquist Court.[^cite_note-tws1nov01-76][^cite_note-tws1nov02-77][^cite_note-nyt-liptak01-78][^cite_note-caplan-amerpros-79] Some of its major rulings have concerned [federal preemption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_preemption) (*[Wyeth v. Levine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyeth_v._Levine)*), [civil procedure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_procedure_in_the_United_States) (*[Twombly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bell_Atlantic_Corp._v._Twombly)–[Iqbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashcroft_v._Iqbal)*), voting rights and federal preclearance (*[Shelby County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shelby_County_v._Holder)*), abortion (*[Gonzales v. Carhart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gonzales_v._Carhart)* and *[Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization)*),[^cite_note-tws1nov04-80] [climate change](5042951.md) (*[Massachusetts v. EPA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_v._Environmental_Protection_Agency)*), [same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States) (*[United States v. Windsor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Windsor)* and *[Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges)*), and the Bill of Rights, such as in *[Citizens United v. Federal Election Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens_United_v._Federal_Election_Commission)* ([First Amendment](31653.md)),[^cite_note-81] *[Heller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_v._Heller)–[McDonald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonald_v._Chicago)–[Bruen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_State_Rifle_&_Pistol_Association,_Inc._v._Bruen)* ([Second Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution)),[^cite_note-tws1nov13-82] and *[Baze v. Rees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baze_v._Rees)* ([Eighth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eighth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution)).[^cite_note-tws1nov21-83][^cite_note-tws1nov22-84]

## Composition

### Nomination, confirmation, and appointment

Main article: [Nomination and confirmation to the Supreme Court of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomination_and_confirmation_to_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States)



[John Roberts](1928850.md) giving testimony before the Senate Judiciary Committee during the [2005 hearings on his nomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Roberts_Supreme_Court_nomination#Confirmation_hearings) to be chief justice

Article II, Section 2, Clause 2 of the [United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Constitution), known as the [Appointments Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appointments_Clause), empowers the [president](24113.md) to nominate and, with the confirmation ([advice and consent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advice_and_consent)) of the United States Senate, to appoint public [officials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official), including justices of the Supreme Court. This clause is one example of the system of checks and balances inherent in the Constitution. The president has the [plenary power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plenary_power) to nominate, while the Senate possesses the plenary power to reject or confirm the nominee. The Constitution sets no qualifications for service as a justice, such as age, citizenship, residence or prior judicial experience, thus a president may nominate anyone to serve, and the Senate may not set any qualifications or otherwise limit who the president can choose.[^cite_note-85] Nonetheless, the Senate may deny confirmation to a candidate that it deems unqualified or unsuitable for the appointment.[^cite_note-86]

In modern times, the confirmation process has attracted considerable attention from the press and advocacy groups, which [lobby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobbying) senators to confirm or to reject a nominee depending on whether their track record aligns with the group's views. The [Senate Judiciary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_the_Judiciary) conducts hearings and votes on whether the nomination should go to the full Senate with a positive, negative or neutral report. The committee's practice of personally interviewing nominees is relatively recent. The first nominee to appear before the committee was [Harlan Fiske Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlan_Fiske_Stone) in 1925, who sought to quell concerns about his links to [Wall Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street), and the modern practice of questioning began with [John Marshall Harlan II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Marshall_Harlan_II) in 1955.[^cite_note-87] Once the committee reports out the nomination, the full Senate considers it. Rejections are relatively uncommon; the Senate has explicitly rejected [twelve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_failed_nominations_to_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States) Supreme Court nominees, most recently [Robert Bork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Bork), nominated by President [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) in 1987.

Although Senate rules do not necessarily allow a negative or tied vote in committee to block a nomination, prior to 2017 a nomination could be blocked by [filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster) once debate had begun in the full Senate. President [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson)'s nomination of sitting associate justice [Abe Fortas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abe_Fortas) to succeed [Earl Warren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earl_Warren) as Chief Justice in 1968 was the first successful filibuster of a Supreme Court nominee. It included both Republican and Democratic senators concerned with Fortas's ethics. President [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s nomination of [Neil Gorsuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Gorsuch) to the seat left vacant by [Antonin Scalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonin_Scalia)'s death was the second. Unlike the Fortas filibuster, only Democratic senators voted against [cloture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloture#United_States) on the Gorsuch nomination, citing his perceived conservative judicial philosophy, and the Republican majority's prior refusal to take up President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama)'s [nomination of Merrick Garland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merrick_Garland_Supreme_Court_nomination) to fill the vacancy.[^cite_note-88] This led the Republican majority to change the rules and eliminate the filibuster for Supreme Court nominations.[^cite_note-89]



[Ruth Bader Ginsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruth_Bader_Ginsburg) giving testimony before the Senate Judiciary Committee during the 1993 hearings on her nomination to be an associate justice

Not every Supreme Court [nominee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nominee#Noun) has received a floor vote in the Senate. A president may withdraw a nomination before an actual confirmation vote occurs, typically because it is clear that the Senate will reject the nominee; this occurred with President George W. Bush's nomination of [Harriet Miers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harriet_Miers) in 2005. The Senate may also fail to act on a nomination, which expires at the end of the session. President [Dwight Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_Eisenhower)'s first nomination of [John Marshall Harlan II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Marshall_Harlan_II) in November 1954 was not acted on by the Senate; Eisenhower re-nominated Harlan in January 1955, and Harlan was confirmed two months later. Most recently, the Senate failed to act on the March 2016 nomination of Merrick Garland, as the nomination expired in January 2017, and the vacancy was filled by Neil Gorsuch, an [appointee](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/appointee) of President Trump.[^cite_note-SCnoms-14]

Once the Senate confirms a nomination, the president must prepare and sign a commission, to which the Seal of the [Department of Justice](52563.md) must be affixed, before the appointee can take office.[^cite_note-90] The seniority of an associate justice is based on the commissioning date, not the confirmation or swearing-in date.[^cite_note-91] After receiving their commission, the appointee must then take the two prescribed oaths before assuming their official duties.[^cite_note-92] The importance of the oath taking is underscored by the case of [Edwin M. Stanton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwin_M._Stanton). Although confirmed by the Senate on December 20, 1869, and duly commissioned as an associate justice by President [Ulysses S. Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulysses_S._Grant), Stanton died on December 24, prior to taking the prescribed oaths. He is not, therefore, considered to have been a member of the court.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94]

Before 1981, the approval process of justices was usually rapid. From the [Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Truman) through [Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) administrations, justices were typically approved within one month. From the Reagan administration to the present, the process has taken much longer and some believe this is because Congress sees justices as playing a more political role than in the past.[^cite_note-95] According to the [Congressional Research Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Research_Service), the average number of days from nomination to final Senate vote since 1975 is 67 days (2.2 months), while the median is 71 days (2.3 months).[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97]

#### Recess appointments

When the Senate is in [recess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recess_\(motion\)), a president may make temporary appointments to fill vacancies. [Recess appointees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recess_appointment) hold office only until the end of the next Senate session (less than two years). The Senate must confirm the nominee for them to continue serving; of the two chief justices and eleven associate justices who have received recess appointments, only Chief Justice [John Rutledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Rutledge) was not subsequently confirmed.[^cite_note-98]

No U.S. president since [Dwight D. Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower) has made a recess appointment to the court, and the practice has become rare and controversial even in lower federal courts.[^cite_note-99] In 1960, after Eisenhower had made three such appointments, the Senate passed a "sense of the Senate" resolution that recess appointments to the court should only be made in "unusual circumstances";[^cite_note-recessapp-100] such resolutions are not legally binding but are an expression of Congress's views in the hope of guiding executive action.[^cite_note-recessapp-100][^cite_note-101]

The Supreme Court's 2014 decision in *[National Labor Relations Board v. Noel Canning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Labor_Relations_Board_v._Noel_Canning)* limited the ability of the president to make recess appointments (including appointments to the Supreme Court); the court ruled that the Senate decides when the Senate is in session or in recess. Writing for the court, Justice Breyer stated, "We hold that, for purposes of the Recess Appointments Clause, the Senate is in session when it says it is, provided that, under its own rules, it retains the capacity to transact Senate business."[^cite_note-102] This ruling allows the Senate to prevent recess appointments through the use of [pro-forma sessions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro_forma#United_States).[^cite_note-103]

### Tenure

&#x20;Lifetime tenure of justices can only be found for US federal judges and the State of Rhode Island's Supreme Court justices, with all other democratic nations and all other US states having set term limits or mandatory retirement ages.[^cite_note-104] [Larry Sabato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Sabato) wrote: "The insularity of lifetime tenure, combined with the appointments of relatively young attorneys who give long service on the bench, produces senior judges representing the views of past generations better than views of the current day."[^cite_note-tws23oct16-105] [Sanford Levinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanford_Levinson) has been critical of justices who stayed in office despite medical deterioration based on longevity.[^cite_note-tws10oct12-106] [James MacGregor Burns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_MacGregor_Burns) stated lifelong tenure has "produced a critical time lag, with the Supreme Court institutionally almost always behind the times".[^cite_note-tws27oct304-107] Proposals to solve these problems include [term limits](505825.md) for justices, as proposed by Levinson[^cite_note-tws10octxx-108] and Sabato[^cite_note-tws23oct16-105][^cite_note-109] and a mandatory retirement age proposed by [Richard Epstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Allen_Epstein),[^cite_note-110] among others.[^cite_note-111] [Alexander Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton) in *[Federalist 78](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalist_78)* argued that one benefit of lifetime tenure was that, "nothing can contribute so much to its firmness and independence as permanency in office".[^cite_note-tws28oct707-112]

The interior of the United States Supreme Court

The interior of the United States Supreme Court

[Article Three, Section 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Behavior_Clause) of the Constitution provides that justices "shall hold their offices during good behavior", which is understood to mean that they may serve for the remainder of their lives, until death; furthermore, the phrase is generally interpreted to mean that the only way justices can be removed from office is by [Congress](31756.md) via the [impeachment process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_impeachment_in_the_United_States). The Framers of the Constitution chose good behavior tenure to limit the power to remove justices and to ensure [judicial independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_independence).[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115] No constitutional mechanism exists for removing a justice who is permanently incapacitated by illness or injury, but unable (or unwilling) to resign.[^cite_note-hufpost-116] The only justice ever to be impeached was [Samuel Chase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Chase), in 1804. The [House of Representatives](19468510.md) adopted eight articles of impeachment against him; however, he was acquitted by the Senate, and remained in office until his death in 1811.[^cite_note-SCchase-117] Two justices, [William O. Douglas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_O._Douglas) and [Abe Fortas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abe_Fortas) were subjected to hearings from the Judiciary Committee, with Douglas being the subject of hearings twice, in 1953 and again in 1970 and Fortas resigned while hearings were being organized in 1969. On July 10, 2024, Representative [Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria_Ocasio-Cortez) filed Articles of Impeachment against justices [Clarence Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Thomas) and [Samuel Alito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Alito), citing their "widely documented financial and personal entanglements".[^cite_note-Impeach-118]

Because justices have indefinite tenure, timing of vacancies can be unpredictable. Sometimes they arise in quick succession, as in September 1971, when [Hugo Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Black) and [John Marshall Harlan II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Marshall_Harlan_II) left within days of each other, the shortest period of time between vacancies in the court's history.[^cite_note-119] Sometimes a great length of time passes between vacancies, such as the 11-year span, from 1994 to 2005, from the retirement of [Harry Blackmun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Blackmun) to the death of [William Rehnquist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rehnquist), which was the second longest timespan between vacancies in the court's history.[^cite_note-120] On average a new justice joins the court about every two years.[^cite_note-SCinstitution-13]

Despite the variability, all but four presidents have been able to appoint at least one justice. [William Henry Harrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison) died a month after taking office, although his successor ([John Tyler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Tyler)) made an appointment during that presidential term. Likewise, [Zachary Taylor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zachary_Taylor) died 16 months after taking office, but his successor ([Millard Fillmore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millard_Fillmore)) also made a Supreme Court nomination before the end of that term. Andrew Johnson, who became president after the [assassination of Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Abraham_Lincoln), was denied the opportunity to appoint a justice by a reduction in the [size of the court](#Size_of_the_court). [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) is the only person elected president to have left office after at least one full term without having the opportunity to appoint a justice. Presidents [James Monroe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Monroe), Franklin D. Roosevelt, and George W. Bush each served a full term without an opportunity to appoint a justice, but made appointments during their subsequent terms in office. No president who has served more than one full term has gone without at least one opportunity to make an appointment.

### Size of the court

The U.S. Supreme Court consists of nine members: one chief justice and eight associate justices. The U.S. Constitution does not specify the size of the Supreme Court, nor does it specify any specific positions for the court's members. The Constitution assumes the existence of the office of the chief justice, because it mentions in [Article I, Section 3, Clause 6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_One_of_the_United_States_Constitution#Clause_6:_Trial_of_Impeachment) that "the Chief Justice" must preside over [impeachment](15328.md) trials of the [president of the United States](24113.md). The power to define the Supreme Court's size and membership has been assumed to belong to Congress, which initially established a six-member Supreme Court composed of a chief justice and five associate justices through the [Judiciary Act of 1789](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary_Act_of_1789).

The size of the court was first altered by the [Midnight Judges Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midnight_Judges_Act) of 1801 which would have reduced the size of the court to five members upon its next vacancy (as federal judges have [life tenure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_tenure)), but the [Judiciary Act of 1802](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary_Act_of_1802) promptly negated the 1801 act, restoring the court's size to six members before any such vacancy occurred. As the nation's boundaries grew across [North America](21139.md) and as Supreme Court justices in those days had to [ride the circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circuit_riding), an arduous process requiring long travel on [horseback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horseback) or [carriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carriage) over harsh terrain that resulted in months-long extended stays away from home, Congress added justices to correspond with the growth. Through this process, the number of seats for associate justices plus the chief justice became [seven in 1807](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventh_Circuit_Act_of_1807), [nine in 1837](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eighth_and_Ninth_Circuits_Act_of_1837), and [ten in 1863](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenth_Circuit_Act_of_1863).[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122]

At the behest of Chief Justice [Chase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salmon_P._Chase), and in an attempt by the Republican Congress to limit the power of Democrat [Andrew Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Johnson), Congress passed the [Judicial Circuits Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_Circuits_Act) of 1866, providing that the next three justices to retire would not be replaced, which would thin the bench to seven justices by attrition. Consequently, one seat was removed in 1866 and a second in 1867. Soon after Johnson left office, the new president [Ulysses S. Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulysses_S._Grant),[^cite_note-123] a Republican, signed into law the [Judiciary Act of 1869](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary_Act_of_1869). This changed the number of justices to nine[^cite_note-124] (where it has since remained), which allowed Grant to fill an additional seat.

President [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) attempted to expand the court in 1937. His proposal envisioned the appointment of one additional justice for each incumbent justice who reached the age of 70 years 6 months and refused retirement, up to a maximum bench of 15 justices. The proposal was ostensibly to ease the burden of the [docket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Docket_\(court\)) on elderly judges, but the actual purpose was widely understood as an effort to "pack" the court with justices who would support Roosevelt's New Deal.[^cite_note-125] The plan, usually called the "[court-packing plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_Procedures_Reform_Bill_of_1937)", failed in Congress after members of Roosevelt's own [Democratic Party](5043544.md) believed it to be unconstitutional. It was defeated 70–20 in the Senate, and the [Senate Judiciary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_the_Judiciary) reported that it was "essential to the continuance of our constitutional democracy" that the proposal "be so emphatically rejected that its parallel will never again be presented to the free representatives of the free people of America."[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129]

The expansion of a 5–4 conservative majority to a 6–3 supermajority during the [first presidency of Donald Trump](52231341.md) led to analysts calling the court the most conservative since the 1930s as well as calls for an expansion in the court's size to fix what some saw as an imbalance, with [Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_\(US\)) having appointed 14 of the 18 justices immediately preceding [Amy Coney Barrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett).[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131] In April 2021, during the [117th Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/117th_United_States_Congress), some Democrats in the [House of Representatives](19468510.md) introduced the Judiciary Act of 2021, a bill to expand the Supreme Court from nine to 13 seats. It met divided views within the party, and Speaker of the House [Nancy Pelosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Pelosi) did not bring it to the floor for a vote.[^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133] Shortly after taking office in January 2021, President [Joe Biden](145422.md) established a [presidential commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Commission_on_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States) to study possible reforms to the Supreme Court. The commission's December 2021 final report discussed, but took no position on, expanding the size of the court.[^cite_note-134]

Lawyer and legal scholar [Jonathan Turley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Turley) has advocated for 19 justices, with the court being gradually expanded by two new members per presidential term, bringing the U.S. Supreme Court to a similar size as its counterparts in other developed countries. He says that a bigger court would reduce the power of the [swing justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swing_vote), ensure the court has "a greater diversity of views", and make confirmation of new justices less politically contentious.[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136]

## Membership

See also: [List of justices of the Supreme Court of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_justices_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States)

### Sitting justices

There are nine justices on the Supreme Court: Chief Justice [John Roberts](1928850.md) and eight associate justices. Clarence Thomas is the longest-serving justice, with a tenure of 34 years, 345 days as of October 3, 2026. The most recent justice to join the court is Ketanji Brown Jackson, whose tenure began on June 30, 2022, after being confirmed by the Senate on April 7.[^cite_note-137]

### Court demographics

Main article: [Demographics of the Supreme Court of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States)

The court has five male and four female justices. Among the nine justices, there are two [African American](2154.md) justices (Justices [Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Thomas) and [Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketanji_Brown_Jackson)) and one [Hispanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic) justice (Justice [Sotomayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonia_Sotomayor)). One of the justices was born to at least one [immigrant](4599312.md) parent: [Justice Alito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Alito)'s father was born in Italy.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140]

At least six justices are [Roman Catholics](606848.md), one is [Jewish](15624.md), and one is [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant). It is unclear whether [Neil Gorsuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Gorsuch) considers himself a Catholic or an [Episcopalian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopalian).[^cite_note-gorsuchreligion-141] Historically, most justices have been Protestants, including 36 Episcopalians, 19 [Presbyterians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterian), 10 [Unitarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitarianism), 5 [Methodists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodist), and 3 [Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptists).[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143] The first Catholic justice was [Roger Taney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_B._Taney) in 1836,[^cite_note-144] and 1916 saw the appointment of the first Jewish justice, [Louis Brandeis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Brandeis).[^cite_note-SCOTUS_FAQ-145] In recent years the historical situation has reversed, as most recent justices have been either Catholic or Jewish.

Three justices are from the state of New York, two are from Washington, D.C., and one each is from New Jersey, Georgia, Colorado, and Louisiana.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148] Eight of the justices received their [Juris Doctor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juris_Doctor) from an [Ivy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivy_League) [law school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_school_in_the_United_States): Neil Gorsuch, Ketanji Brown Jackson, [Elena Kagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elena_Kagan) and [John Roberts](1928850.md) from [Harvard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_Law_School); plus Samuel Alito, [Brett Kavanaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brett_Kavanaugh), Sonia Sotomayor and Clarence Thomas from [Yale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Law_School). Only [Amy Coney Barrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett) did not; she received her Juris Doctor at [Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notre_Dame_Law_School).

Previous positions or offices, judicial or federal government, prior to joining the court (by order of seniority following the chief justice) include:

The first four female justices: O'Connor, Sotomayor, Ginsburg, and Kagan

For much of the court's history, every justice was a man of [Northwestern European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Europe) descent, and almost always [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant). Diversity concerns focused on geography, to represent all [regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regionalism_\(politics\)) of the country, rather than religious, ethnic, or gender diversity.[^cite_note-obrien46-149] Racial, ethnic, and gender diversity in the court increased in the late 20th century. [Thurgood Marshall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thurgood_Marshall) became the first [African-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American) justice in 1967.[^cite_note-SCOTUS_FAQ-145] [Sandra Day O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandra_Day_O'Connor) became the first female justice in 1981.[^cite_note-SCOTUS_FAQ-145] In 1986, [Antonin Scalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonin_Scalia) became the first [Italian-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian-American) justice. Marshall was succeeded by African-American Clarence Thomas in 1991.[^cite_note-150] O'Connor was joined by [Ruth Bader Ginsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruth_Bader_Ginsburg), the first Jewish woman on the Court, in 1993.[^cite_note-Four_Justices-151] After O'Connor's retirement, Ginsburg was joined in 2009 by [Sonia Sotomayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonia_Sotomayor), the first [Hispanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic) and [Latina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latinas) justice,[^cite_note-SCOTUS_FAQ-145] and in 2010 by Elena Kagan.[^cite_note-Four_Justices-151] After Ginsburg's death on September 18, 2020, Amy Coney Barrett was confirmed as the fifth woman in the court's history on October 26, 2020. Ketanji Brown Jackson is the sixth woman and first African-American woman on the court.

There have been six foreign-born justices in the court's history: James Wilson (1789–1798), born in [Caskardy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceres,_Fife), Scotland; [James Iredell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Iredell) (1790–1799), born in [Lewes](67930.md), England; [William Paterson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Paterson_\(judge\)) (1793–1806), born in [County Antrim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_Antrim), Ireland; [David Brewer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Josiah_Brewer) (1889–1910), born to American missionaries in [Smyrna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smyrna), [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) (now [İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir), Turkey); [George Sutherland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Sutherland) (1922–1939), born in [Buckinghamshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buckinghamshire), England; and [Felix Frankfurter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felix_Frankfurter) (1939–1962), born in [Vienna](55866.md), [Austria-Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austria-Hungary) (later Austria).[^cite_note-SCOTUS_FAQ-145]

Since 1789, about one-third of the justices have been [U.S. military](32212.md) veterans. Samuel Alito is the only veteran serving on the court.[^cite_note-152] Retired justices Stephen Breyer and Anthony Kennedy also served in the U.S. military.[^cite_note-153]

### Judicial leanings

Further information: [Ideological leanings of United States Supreme Court justices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideological_leanings_of_United_States_Supreme_Court_justices)

Justices are nominated by the president in power, and receive confirmation by the Senate, historically holding many of the views of the nominating president's political party. While justices do not represent or receive official endorsements from political parties, as is accepted practice in the legislative and executive branches, advocacy groups have played a role in the selection, nomination, and confirmation process.[^cite_note-154] The justices are often informally categorized in the media as being conservatives or liberal. Attempts to quantify the ideologies of jurists include the [Segal–Cover score](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Segal%E2%80%93Cover_score), [Martin–Quinn score](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin%E2%80%93Quinn_score), and [Judicial Common Space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_Common_Space) score.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156]

Devins and Baum argue that before 2010, the Court never had clear ideological blocs that fell perfectly along party lines. In choosing their appointments, Presidents often focused more on friendship and political connections than on ideology. Republican presidents sometimes appointed liberals, and Democratic presidents sometimes appointed conservatives. As a result, "... between 1790 and early 2010 there were only two decisions that the *Guide to the U.S. Supreme Court* designated as important and that had at least two dissenting votes in which the justices divided along party lines, about one-half of one percent."[^cite_note-DevinsBaum-157]<sup>: 316 </sup>[^cite_note-DevinsBaumBook-158] Even in the turbulent 1960s and 1970s, Democratic and Republican elites tended to agree on some major issues, especially concerning civil rights and civil liberties—and so did the justices. But since 1991, they argue, ideology has been much more important in choosing justices—all Republican appointees have been committed conservatives, and all Democratic appointees have been liberals.[^cite_note-DevinsBaum-157]<sup>: 331–344 </sup> As the more moderate Republican justices retired, the court has become more partisan. The Court became more sharply divided along partisan lines, with justices appointed by Republican presidents taking increasingly conservative positions and those appointed by Democrats taking increasingly liberal positions.[^cite_note-DevinsBaum-157]<sup>: 357 </sup>



Balance of the US Supreme Court since 2020, shaded by party of the nominating president: Blue represents a [Democratic](5043544.md) president and red a [Republican](32070.md) president

Following the confirmation of [Amy Coney Barrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett) in 2020 after the death of [Ruth Bader Ginsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruth_Bader_Ginsburg), the court is composed of six justices appointed by Republican presidents and three appointed by Democratic presidents. It is popularly accepted that Chief Justice [Roberts](1928850.md) and associate justices [Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Thomas), [Alito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Alito), [Gorsuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Gorsuch), [Kavanaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brett_Kavanaugh), and Barrett, appointed by Republican presidents, compose the court's conservative wing, and that justices [Sotomayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonia_Sotomayor), [Kagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elena_Kagan), and [Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketanji_Brown_Jackson), appointed by Democratic presidents, compose the court's liberal wing.[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160] Prior to Justice Ginsburg's death in 2020, the conservative chief justice Roberts was sometimes described as the court's 'median justice' (with four justices more liberal and four more conservative than he is).[^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162] Darragh Roche argues that Kavanaugh as 2021's median justice exemplifies the rightward shift in the court.[^cite_note-median-163] [Noah Feldman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noah_Feldman) describes the current ideological makeup of the court as a three-way split between the liberal faction, centrist-conservative faction, and arch-conservative faction, each with three members.[^cite_note-164]

*[FiveThirtyEight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FiveThirtyEight)* found the number of unanimous decisions dropped from the 20-year average of nearly 50% to nearly 30% in 2021 while party-line rulings increased from a 60-year average just above zero to a record high 21%.[^cite_note-165] That year Ryan Williams pointed to the party-line votes for confirmations of justices as evidence that the court is of partisan importance to the Senate.[^cite_note-166] In 2022, Simon Lazarus of Brookings critiqued the U.S. Supreme Court as an increasingly partisan institution.[^cite_note-167] A 2024 AP-NORC poll showed 7 in 10 respondents believed the court decides cases to "fit their own ideologies" as opposed to "acting as an independent check on other branches of government by being fair and impartial."[^cite_note-168]

### Retired justices

There are two living retired justices of the Supreme Court of the United States: Anthony Kennedy and Stephen Breyer. As retired justices, they no longer participate in the work of the Supreme Court, but may be designated for temporary assignments to sit on lower federal courts, usually the [United States Courts of Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Courts_of_Appeals). Such assignments are formally made by the chief justice, on request of the [chief judge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_judge_\(United_States\)) of the lower court and with the consent of the retired justice. The status of a retired justice is analogous to that of a circuit or district court judge who has taken [senior status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senior_status), and eligibility of a Supreme Court justice to assume retired status (rather than simply resign from the bench) is governed by the same age and service criteria.

In recent times, justices tend to strategically plan their decisions to leave the bench with personal, institutional, ideological, partisan, and political factors playing a role.[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170] The fear of mental decline and death often motivates justices to step down. The desire to maximize the court's strength and legitimacy through one retirement at a time, when the court is in recess and during non-presidential election years suggests a concern for institutional health. Finally, especially in recent decades, many justices have timed their departure to coincide with a philosophically compatible president holding office, to ensure that a like-minded successor would be appointed.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172]

### Salary

Further information: [Federal judge salaries in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_judge_salaries_in_the_United_States)

See also: [Supreme Court of the United States § Ethics](#Ethics)

As of 2024, associate justices receive a yearly salary of \$298,500 and the chief justice is paid \$312,200 per year.[^cite_note-salary-173] Once a justice meets [age and service requirements](834368.md#Retirement), the justice may retire with a pension based on the same formula used for federal employees. As with other federal courts judges, their pension can never be less than their salary at the time of retirement according to the [Compensation Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compensation_Clause) of [Article III of the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_Three_of_the_United_States_Constitution).[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175]

### Seniority and seating



The Roberts Court (since June 2022): Front row (left to right): [Sonia Sotomayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonia_Sotomayor), [Clarence Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Thomas), Chief Justice [John Roberts](1928850.md), [Samuel Alito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Alito), and [Elena Kagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elena_Kagan). Back row (left to right): [Amy Coney Barrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett), [Neil Gorsuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Gorsuch), [Brett Kavanaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brett_Kavanaugh), and [Ketanji Brown Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketanji_Brown_Jackson).

For the most part, the day-to-day activities of the justices are governed by rules of protocol based upon the [seniority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seniority) of justices. The chief justice always ranks first in the [order of precedence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_precedence)—regardless of the length of their service.[^cite_note-176] The associate justices are then ranked by the length of their service.[^cite_note-177] The chief justice sits in the center on the bench, or at the head of the table during conferences. The other justices are seated in order of seniority. The senior-most associate justice sits immediately to the chief justice's right; the second most senior sits immediately to their left. The seats alternate right to left in order of seniority, with the most junior justice occupying the last seat.[^cite_note-178] Therefore, since the October 2022 term, the court sits in order from left to right, from the perspective of those facing the court: Barrett, Gorsuch, Sotomayor, Thomas (most senior associate justice), Roberts (chief justice), Alito, Kagan, Kavanaugh, and Jackson.[^cite_note-179] Likewise, when the members of the court gather for official group photographs, justices are arranged in order of seniority, with the five most senior members seated in the front row in the same order as they would sit during Court sessions (currently, from left to right, Sotomayor, Thomas, Roberts, Alito, and Kagan), and the four most junior justices standing behind them, again in the same order as they would sit during Court sessions (Barrett, Gorsuch, Kavanaugh, and Jackson).[^cite_note-180]

In the justices' private conferences, the practice is for them to speak and vote in order of seniority, beginning with the chief justice and ending with the most junior associate justice. By custom, the most junior associate justice in these conferences is charged with any menial tasks the justices may require as they convene alone, such as answering the door of their conference room, serving beverages and transmitting orders of the court to the clerk.[^cite_note-181]

## Facilities

Main article: [United States Supreme Court Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Supreme_Court_Building)



From the 1860s until the 1930s, the court sat in the [Old Senate Chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Senate_Chamber) of the [U.S. Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Capitol).



Current US Supreme Court building in 2007

The Supreme Court first met on February 1, 1790, at the Merchants' Exchange Building in New York City. When Philadelphia became the capital, the court met briefly in Independence Hall before settling in [Old City Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_City_Hall_\(Philadelphia\)) from 1791 until 1800. After the government moved to Washington, D.C., the court occupied various spaces in the Capitol building until 1935, when it moved into its own purpose-built home. The four-story building was designed by [Cass Gilbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cass_Gilbert) in a classical style sympathetic to the surrounding buildings of the Capitol and [Library of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Congress), and is clad in marble. The building includes the courtroom, justices' chambers, an extensive [law library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_library), various meeting spaces, and auxiliary services including a gymnasium. The Supreme Court building is within the ambit of the [Architect of the Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architect_of_the_Capitol), but maintains its own [Supreme Court Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_Police), separate from the [Capitol Police](1915214.md).[^cite_note-tws24oct204-182]

Located across First Street from the United States Capitol at One First Street NE and Maryland Avenue,[^cite_note-tws24oct201-183][^cite_note-tws24oct211-184] the building is open to the public. Visitors may not tour the actual courtroom unaccompanied. There is a cafeteria, a gift shop, exhibits, and a half-hour informational film. When the court is not in session, lectures about the courtroom are held hourly from 9\:30 am to 3\:30 pm and reservations are not necessary.[^cite_note-tws24oct204-182] When the court is in session the public may attend oral arguments, which are held twice each morning (and sometimes afternoons) on Mondays, Tuesdays, and Wednesdays in two-week intervals from October through late April, with breaks during December and February. Visitors are seated on a first-come first-served basis. One estimate is there are about 250 seats available.[^cite_note-tws24oct209-185] The number of open seats varies from case to case; for important cases, some visitors arrive the day before and wait through the night. The court releases opinions beginning at 10 am on scheduled "non-argument days" (also called opinion days)[^cite_note-cal-186] These sessions, which typically last 15 to 30-minute, are also open to the public.[^cite_note-cal-186][^cite_note-tws24oct204-182] From mid-May until the end of June, at least one opinion day is scheduled each week.[^cite_note-tws24oct204-182] Supreme Court Police are available to answer questions.[^cite_note-tws24oct201-183]
