Five of the current justices are assigned to circuits on which they previously sat as circuit judges: Chief Justice Roberts (D.C. Circuit), Justice Sotomayor (Second Circuit), Justice Alito (Third Circuit), Justice Barrett (Seventh Circuit), and Justice Gorsuch (Tenth Circuit). Justices Thomas, Kavanaugh, and Jackson all sat as circuit judges on the D.C. Circuit, which is assigned to Chief Justice Roberts. Justice Kagan is the only current member who did not serve as a judge prior to their appointment to the Court.

## Process

Main article: [Procedures of the Supreme Court of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Procedures_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States)

### Case selection

Nearly all cases come before the court by way of petitions for writs of *[certiorari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Certiorari)*, commonly referred to as *cert*, upon which the court grants a writ of certiorari. The court may review via this process any civil or criminal case in the federal courts of appeals.[^cite_note-usc|28|1254-195] It may also review by certiorari a final judgment of the highest court of a state if the judgment involves a question of federal statutory or constitutional law.[^cite_note-209] A case may alternatively come before the court as a direct appeal from a three-judge federal district court.[^cite_note-210] The party that petitions the court for review is the *[petitioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petitioner)* and the non-mover is the *respondent*.

Case names before the court are styled *petitioner* v. *respondent*, regardless of which party initiated the lawsuit in the trial court. For example, criminal prosecutions are brought in the name of the state and against an individual, as in *State of Arizona v. Ernesto Miranda*. If the defendant is convicted, and his conviction then is affirmed on appeal in the [state supreme court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_supreme_court), when he petitions for cert the name of the case becomes *Miranda v. Arizona*.

The court also hears questions submitted to it by appeals courts themselves via a process known as certification.[^cite_note-usc|28|1254-195]

The Supreme Court relies on the record assembled by lower courts for the facts of a case and deals solely with [the question of how the law applies to the facts presented](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Question_of_law). There are however [situations where the court has original jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Original_jurisdiction_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States), such as when two states have a dispute against each other, or when there is a dispute between the United States and a state. In such instances, a case is filed with the Supreme Court directly. Examples of such cases include *United States v. Texas*, a case to determine whether a parcel of land belonged to the United States or to Texas, and *[Virginia v. Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_v._Tennessee)*, a case turning on whether an incorrectly drawn boundary between two states can be changed by a state court, and whether the setting of the correct boundary requires Congressional approval. Although it has not happened since 1794 in the case of *[Georgia v. Brailsford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_v._Brailsford_\(1794\))*,[^cite_note-211] parties in an action at law in which the Supreme Court has original jurisdiction may request that a [jury](44720.md) determine issues of fact.[^cite_note-212] *Georgia v. Brailsford* remains the only case in which the court has [empaneled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_panel) a jury, in this case a [special jury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_jury).[^cite_note-yalejournal-213] Two other original jurisdiction cases involve colonial era borders and rights under navigable waters in *[New Jersey v. Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Jersey_v._Delaware)*, and water rights between [riparian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riparian_water_rights#General_Principle) states upstream of navigable waters in *[Kansas v. Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_v._Colorado)*.

A cert petition is voted on at a session of the court called conference. A conference is a private meeting of the nine justices by themselves; the public and the justices' clerks are excluded. The [rule of four](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule_of_four) permits four of the nine justices to grant a writ of *certiorari*. If it is granted, the case proceeds to the briefing stage; otherwise, the case ends. Except in [death penalty](5902.md) cases and other cases in which the court orders briefing from the respondent, the respondent may, but is not required to, file a response to the cert petition. The court grants a petition for cert only for "compelling reasons", spelled out in the court's Rule 10. Such reasons include:

- Resolving a conflict between circuit courts in the interpretation of a federal law or a provision of the federal Constitution

- Correcting an egregious departure from the accepted and usual course of judicial proceedings

- Resolving an important question of federal law, or to expressly review a decision of a lower court that conflicts directly with a previous decision of the court.

When a conflict of interpretations arises from differing interpretations of the same law or constitutional provision issued by different federal circuit courts of appeals, lawyers call this situation a "[circuit split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circuit_split)"; if the court votes to deny a cert petition, as it does in the vast majority of such petitions that come before it, it does so typically without comment. A denial of a cert petition is not a judgment on the merits of a case, and the decision of the lower court stands as the case's final ruling. To manage the high volume of cert petitions received by the court each year (of the more than 7,000 petitions the court receives each year, it will usually request briefing and hear oral argument in 100 or fewer), the court employs an internal case management tool known as the "[cert pool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cert_pool)"; currently, all justices except for Justices Alito and Gorsuch participate in the cert pool.[^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217]

### Written evidence

The Court also relies on and cites [amicus briefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amicus_briefs), [law review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_review) articles, and other written works for their decisions. While law review article use has increased slightly with one article cited per decision on average,[^cite_note-218] the use of amicus briefs has increased significantly.[^cite_note-\:4-219] The use of amicus briefs has received criticism, including the ability of authors to discuss topics outside their expertise (unlike in lower courts),[^cite_note-\:4-219] with documented examples of falsehoods in written opinions, often supplied to the justices by amicus briefs from groups advocating a particular outcome.[^cite_note-\:5-220] The lack of funding transparency and the lack of a requirement to submit them earlier in the process also make it more difficult to [fact-check](595273.md) and understand the credibility of amicus briefs.[^cite_note-\:4-219]

### Oral argument

A man speaking at a lectern before two supreme court justices.

[Seth P. Waxman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seth_P._Waxman) at oral argument presents his case and answers questions from the justices.

When the court grants a cert petition, the case is set for oral argument. Both parties will file briefs on the merits of the case, as distinct from the reasons they may have argued for granting or denying the cert petition. With the consent of the parties or approval of the court, *[amici curiae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amicus_curiae)*, or "friends of the court", may also file briefs. The court holds two-week oral argument sessions each month from October through April. Each side has thirty minutes to present its argument (the court may choose to give more time, although this is rare),[^cite_note-221] and during that time, the justices may interrupt the advocate and ask questions.

In 2019, the court adopted a rule generally allowing advocates to speak uninterrupted for the first two minutes of their argument.[^cite_note-222] The petitioner gives the first presentation, and may reserve some time to [rebut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebuttal) the respondent's arguments after the respondent has concluded. *Amici curiae* may also present oral argument on behalf of one party if that party agrees. The court advises counsel to assume that the justices are familiar with and have read the briefs filed in a case.

### Decision

At the conclusion of oral argument, the case is submitted for decision. Cases are decided by majority vote of the justices. After the oral argument is concluded, usually in the same week as the case was submitted, the justices retire to another conference at which the preliminary votes are tallied and the court sees which side has prevailed. One of the justices in the majority is then assigned to write the court's opinion, also known as the "majority opinion", an assignment made by the most senior justice in the majority, with the chief justice always being considered the most senior. Drafts of the court's opinion circulate among the justices until the court is prepared to announce the judgment in a particular case.[^cite_note-223]

Justices are free to change their votes on a case up until the decision is finalized and published. In any given case, a justice is free to choose whether or not to author an opinion or else simply join the majority or another justice's opinion. There are several primary types of opinions:

- **Opinion of the court**: this is the binding decision of the Supreme Court. An opinion that more than half of the justices join (usually at least five justices, since there are nine justices in total; but in cases where some justices do not participate it could be fewer) is known as "majority opinion" and creates binding precedent in American law. Whereas an opinion that fewer than half of the justices join is known as a "plurality opinion" and is only partially binding precedent.

- **Concurring**: a justice agrees with and joins the majority opinion but authors a separate concurrence to give additional explanations, rationales, or commentary. Concurrences do not create binding precedent.

- **Concurring in the judgment**: a justice agrees with the outcome the court reached but disagrees with its reasons for doing so. A justice in this situation does not join the majority opinion. Like regular concurrences, these do not create binding precedent.

- **Dissent**: a justice disagrees with the outcome the court reached and its reasoning. Justices who dissent from a decision may author their own dissenting opinions or, if there are multiple dissenting justices in a decision, may join another justice's dissent. Dissents do not create binding precedent. A justice may also join only part(s) of a particular decision, and may even agree with some parts of the outcome and disagree with others.

It is the court's practice to issue decisions in all cases argued in a particular term by the end of that term. Within that term, the court is under no obligation to release a decision within any set time after oral argument. Since recording devices are banned inside the courtroom of the Supreme Court Building, the delivery of the decision to the media has historically been done via paper copies in what was known as the "[Running of the Interns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Running_of_the_Interns)".[^cite_note-224] However, this practice has become passé as the Court now posts electronic copies of the opinions on its website as they are being announced.[^cite_note-225]

It is possible that through recusals or vacancies the court divides evenly on a case. If that occurs, then the decision of the court below is affirmed, but does not establish binding precedent. In effect, it results in a return to the *[status quo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Status_quo) ante*. For a case to be heard, there must be a quorum of at least six justices.[^cite_note-28_USC_1-226] If a quorum is not available to hear a case and a majority of qualified justices believes that the case cannot be heard and determined in the next term, then the judgment of the court below is affirmed as if the court had been evenly divided. For cases brought to the Supreme Court by direct appeal from a United States District Court, the chief justice may order the case [remanded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remand_\(court_procedure\)) to the appropriate U.S. Court of Appeals for a final decision there.[^cite_note-227] This has only occurred once in U.S. history, in the case of *[United States v. Alcoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Alcoa)* (1945).[^cite_note-228]

### Published opinions

|  | This section needs to be **updated**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(August 2021)* |
| --- | --- |

The court's opinions are published in three stages. First, a [slip opinion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slip_opinion) is made available on the court's web site and through other outlets. Next, several opinions and lists of the court's orders are bound together in paperback form, called a preliminary print of *[United States Reports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Reports)*, the official series of books in which the final version of the court's opinions appears.

About a year after the preliminary prints are issued, a final bound volume of *U.S. Reports* is issued by the [Reporter of Decisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reporter_of_Decisions_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States). The individual volumes of *U.S. Reports* are numbered so that users may cite this set of reports, or a competing version published by another commercial legal publisher but containing parallel citations, to allow those who read their pleadings and other briefs to find the cases quickly and easily.

As of January 2019, there are:

- Final bound volumes of *U.S. Reports*: 569 volumes, covering cases to June 13, 2013 (part of the October 2012 term).

  [^cite_note-USRBV-229]

  [^cite_note-USRv569-230]

- [Slip opinions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slip_opinion): 21 volumes (565–585 for 2011–2017 terms, three two-part volumes each), plus part 1 of volume 586 (2018 term).

  [^cite_note-sliplists-231]

As of March 2012, the *U.S. Reports* have published a total of 30,161 Supreme Court opinions, covering the decisions handed down from February 1790 to March 2012. This figure does not reflect the number of cases the court has taken up, as several cases can be addressed by a single opinion. See, for example, *[Parents v. Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parents_Involved_in_Community_Schools_v._Seattle_School_District_No._1)*, where *[Meredith v. Jefferson County Board of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meredith_v._Jefferson_County_Board_of_Education)* was also decided in the same opinion.

By a similar logic, *[Miranda v. Arizona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miranda_v._Arizona)* decided *Miranda* and three other cases: *Vignera v. New York*, *Westover v. United States*, and *California v. Stewart*. A more unusual example is [The Telephone Cases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Telephone_Cases), which are a single set of interlinked opinions that take up the entire 126th volume of the *U.S. Reports*.

Opinions are also collected and published in two unofficial, parallel reporters: *[Supreme Court Reporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_Reporter)*, published by [West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_\(publisher\)) (now a part of [Thomson Reuters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomson_Reuters)), and *[United States Supreme Court Reports, Lawyers' Edition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Supreme_Court_Reports,_Lawyers'_Edition)* (simply known as *Lawyers' Edition*), published by [LexisNexis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LexisNexis). In court documents, legal periodicals and other legal media, case citations generally contain cites from each of the three reporters; for example, citation to *Citizens United v. Federal Election Commission* is presented as *Citizens United v. Federal Election Com'n*, 585 U.S. 50, 130 S. Ct. 876, 175 L. Ed. 2d 753 (2010), with "S. Ct." representing the *Supreme Court Reporter*, and "L. Ed." representing the *Lawyers' Edition*.[^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233]

#### Citations to published opinions

Further information: [Case citation § Supreme Court of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case_citation#Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States)

Lawyers use an abbreviated format to cite cases, in the form "vol U.S. page, pin (year)", where vol is the volume number, page is the page number on which the opinion begins, and year is the year in which the case was decided. Optionally, pin is used to "pinpoint" to a specific page number within the opinion. For instance, the citation for *Roe v. Wade* is 410 U.S. 113 (1973), which means the case was decided in 1973 and appears on page 113 of volume 410 of *U.S. Reports*. For opinions or orders that have not yet been published in the preliminary print, the volume and page numbers may be replaced with *\_\_\_*

### Supreme Court bar

In order to plead before the court, an attorney must first be admitted to the court's bar. Approximately 4,000 lawyers join the bar each year. The bar contains an estimated 230,000 members. In reality, pleading is limited to several hundred attorneys. The rest join for a one-time fee of \$200, with the court collecting about \$750,000 annually. Attorneys can be admitted as either individuals or as groups. The group admission is held before the current justices of the Supreme Court, wherein the chief justice approves a motion to admit the new attorneys.[^cite_note-234] Lawyers commonly apply for the cosmetic value of a certificate to display in their office or on their resume. They also receive access to better seating if they wish to attend an oral argument.[^cite_note-235] Members of the Supreme Court Bar are also granted access to the collections of the Supreme Court Library.[^cite_note-236]

### Term

A term of the Supreme Court commences on the first Monday of each October, and continues until June or early July of the following year. Each term consists of alternating periods of around two weeks known as "sittings" and "recesses"; justices hear cases and deliver rulings during sittings, and discuss cases and write opinions during recesses.[^cite_note-237]

## Institutional powers



Inscription on the wall of the Supreme Court Building from *[Marbury v. Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marbury_v._Madison)*, in which Chief Justice John Marshall outlined the concept of judicial review

The federal court system and the judicial authority to interpret the Constitution received little attention in the debates over the drafting and ratification of the Constitution. The power of [judicial review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_review), in fact, is nowhere mentioned in it. Over the ensuing years, the question of whether the power of judicial review was even intended by the drafters of the Constitution was quickly frustrated by the lack of evidence bearing on the question either way.[^cite_note-google118-238]

Nevertheless, the power of judiciary to overturn laws and executive actions it determines are unlawful or unconstitutional is a well-established precedent. Many of the [Founding Fathers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States) accepted the notion of judicial review; in [Federalist No. 78](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalist_No._78), [Alexander Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton) wrote: "A Constitution is, in fact, and must be regarded by the judges, as a fundamental law. It therefore belongs to them to ascertain its meaning, and the meaning of any particular act proceeding from the legislative body. If there should happen to be an irreconcilable variance between the two, that which has the superior obligation and validity ought, of course, to be preferred; or, in other words, the Constitution ought to be preferred to the statute."

The Supreme Court established its own power to declare laws unconstitutional in *[Marbury v. Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marbury_v._Madison)* (1803), consummating the American system of [checks and balances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_powers#Checks_and_balances). In explaining the power of judicial review, Chief Justice [John Marshall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Marshall) stated that the authority to interpret the law was the particular province of the courts, part of the *duty of the judicial department to say what the law is.* His contention was not that the court had privileged insight into constitutional requirements, but that it was the constitutional duty of the judiciary, as well as the other branches of government, to read and obey the dictates of the Constitution.[^cite_note-google118-238] This decision was criticized by then-President [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson) who said, "the Constitution, on this hypothesis, is a mere thing of wax in the hands of the judiciary, which they may twist and shape into any form they please."[^cite_note-239]

Since the founding of the republic, there has been a tension between the practice of judicial review and the [democratic ideals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_ideals) of [egalitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equality_before_the_law), self-government, self-determination and freedom of conscience. At one pole are those who view the federal judiciary and especially the Supreme Court as being "the most separated and least checked of all branches of government."[^cite_note-240] Federal judges and justices on the Supreme Court are not required to stand for election by virtue of their tenure "during good behavior", and their pay may "not be diminished" while they hold their position ([Section 1 of Article Three](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_Three_of_the_United_States_Constitution#Section_1:_Federal_courts)). Although subject to the process of impeachment, only one justice has ever been impeached and no Supreme Court justice has been removed from office. At the other pole are those who view the judiciary as the least dangerous branch, with little ability to resist the exhortations of the other branches of government.[^cite_note-google118-238]

### Constraints

The Supreme Court cannot directly enforce its rulings; instead, it relies on respect for the Constitution and for the law for adherence to its judgments. Popular history claims an instance of judicial [nonacquiesence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonacquiesence?action=edit\&redlink=1) in 1832, when the state of [Georgia](48830.md) ignored the Supreme Court's decision in *[Worcester v. Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worcester_v._Georgia)*. President [Andrew Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Jackson), who sided with the Georgia courts, is supposed to have remarked, "[John Marshall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Marshall) has made his decision; now let him enforce it!",[^cite_note-241] but the tale is apocryphal.[^cite_note-242]

Some state governments in the [South](179553.md) also resisted the desegregation of public schools after the 1954 judgment *Brown v. Board of Education*. More recently, many feared that President Nixon would refuse to comply with the court's order in *[United States v. Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Nixon)* (1974) to surrender the [Watergate tapes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watergate_tapes).[^cite_note-243] Nixon ultimately complied with the Supreme Court's ruling.[^cite_note-244]

Supreme Court decisions can be purposefully overturned by constitutional amendment, something that has happened on six occasions:[^cite_note-245]

- *[Chisholm v. Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chisholm_v._Georgia)* (1793) – overturned by the [Eleventh Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleventh_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution) (1795)

- *[Dred Scott v. Sandford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dred_Scott_v._Sandford)* (1857) – overturned by the [Thirteenth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution) (1865) and the [Fourteenth Amendment](31666.md) (1868)

- *[Pollock v. Farmers' Loan & Trust Co.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pollock_v._Farmers'_Loan_&_Trust_Co.)* (1895) – overturned by the [Sixteenth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution) (1913)

- *[Minor v. Happersett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor_v._Happersett)* (1875) – overturned by the [Nineteenth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nineteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution) (1920)

- *[Breedlove v. Suttles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breedlove_v._Suttles)* (1937) – overturned by the [Twenty-fourth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-fourth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution) (1964)

- *[Oregon v. Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_v._Mitchell)* (1970) – overturned by the [Twenty-sixth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-sixth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution) (1971)

Recognizing the difficulty of constitutional amendment, and to avoid the antidemocratic problems inherent to the publication of decisions holding legislation or executive actions unconstitutional, the Court has resorted to self-imposed canons of construction and doctrinal rules, such as the doctrine of [constitutional avoidance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_avoidance),[^cite_note-246] to minimize occurrences where the political branches or popular movements should need to reverse the Court via constitutional amendment.

When the court rules on matters involving the interpretation of federal statutes rather than of the Constitution, simple legislative action can reverse the decisions (for example, in 2009 Congress passed the [Lilly Ledbetter Fair Pay Act of 2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lilly_Ledbetter_Fair_Pay_Act_of_2009), superseding the limitations given in *[Ledbetter v. Goodyear Tire & Rubber Co.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ledbetter_v._Goodyear_Tire_&_Rubber_Co.)* in 2007). Also, the Supreme Court is not immune from political and institutional consideration: lower federal courts and state courts sometimes resist doctrinal innovations, as do law enforcement officials.[^cite_note-247]

In addition, the other two branches can restrain the court through other mechanisms. Congress can increase the number of justices, giving the president power to influence future decisions by appointments (as in Roosevelt's court-packing plan discussed above). Congress can pass legislation that [restricts the jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurisdiction_stripping) of the Supreme Court and other federal courts over certain topics and cases: this is suggested by language in [Section 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_3_\(U.S._Constitution\)#Section_2:_Federal_jurisdiction_and_trial_by_jury) of Article Three, where the appellate jurisdiction is granted "with such Exceptions, and under such Regulations as the Congress shall make." The court sanctioned such congressional action in the Reconstruction Era case *[Ex parte McCardle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ex_parte_McCardle)* (1869), although it rejected Congress' power to dictate how particular cases must be decided in *[United States v. Klein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Klein)* (1871).[^cite_note-248]

On the other hand, through its power of judicial review, the Supreme Court has defined the scope and nature of the powers and separation between the legislative and executive branches of the federal government; for example, in *[United States v. Curtiss-Wright Export Corp.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Curtiss-Wright_Export_Corp.)* (1936), *[Dames & Moore v. Regan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dames_&_Moore_v._Regan)* (1981), and notably in *[Goldwater v. Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goldwater_v._Carter)* (1979), which effectively gave the presidency the power to terminate ratified treaties without the consent of Congress. The court's decisions can also impose limitations on the scope of Executive authority, as in *[Humphrey's Executor v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humphrey's_Executor_v._United_States)* (1935), the *[Steel Seizure Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youngstown_Sheet_&_Tube_Co._v._Sawyer)* (1952), and *[United States v. Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Nixon)* (1974).

## Law clerks

Further information: [Lists of law clerks of the Supreme Court of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lists_of_law_clerks_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States)

Each Supreme Court justice hires several [law clerks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_clerk#Federal_clerkships) to review petitions for writ of *[certiorari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Certiorari)*, [research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_research) them, prepare [bench memorandums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bench_memorandum), and draft opinions. Associate justices are allowed four clerks. The chief justice is allowed five clerks, but [Chief Justice Rehnquist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Justice_Rehnquist) hired only three per year, and [Chief Justice Roberts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Justice_Roberts) usually hires only four.[^cite_note-Peppers2006-249] Generally, law clerks serve a term of one to two years.

The first law clerk was hired by Associate Justice [Horace Gray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horace_Gray) in 1882.[^cite_note-Peppers2006-249][^cite_note-Weiden2006-250] [Oliver Wendell Holmes Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Wendell_Holmes_Jr.) and [Louis Brandeis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Brandeis) were the first Supreme Court justices to use recent [law school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_school) graduates as clerks, rather than hiring "a [stenographer](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/stenographer#Noun)-secretary".[^cite_note-Chace2007-251] Most law clerks are recent law school graduates.

The first female clerk was [Lucile Lomen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucile_Lomen), hired in 1944 by Justice [William O. Douglas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_O._Douglas).[^cite_note-Peppers2006-249] The first African-American, [William T. Coleman Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_T._Coleman_Jr.), was hired in 1948 by Justice [Felix Frankfurter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felix_Frankfurter).[^cite_note-Peppers2006-249] A disproportionately large number of law clerks have obtained law degrees from elite law schools, especially Harvard, Yale, the University of Chicago, Columbia, and Stanford. From 1882 to 1940, 62% of law clerks were graduates of Harvard Law School.[^cite_note-Peppers2006-249] Those chosen to be Supreme Court law clerks usually have graduated in the top of their law school class and were often an editor of the [law review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_review) or a member of the [moot court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moot_court) board. By the mid-1970s, clerking previously for a judge in a [federal court of appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Courts_of_Appeals) had also become a prerequisite to clerking for a Supreme Court justice.

Ten Supreme Court justices previously clerked for other justices: [Byron White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byron_White) for [Frederick M. Vinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_M._Vinson), [John Paul Stevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Paul_Stevens) for [Wiley Rutledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wiley_Rutledge), [William Rehnquist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rehnquist) for [Robert H. Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_H._Jackson), Stephen Breyer for [Arthur Goldberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Goldberg), [John Roberts](1928850.md) for [William Rehnquist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rehnquist), [Elena Kagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elena_Kagan) for [Thurgood Marshall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thurgood_Marshall), [Neil Gorsuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Gorsuch) for both [Byron White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byron_White) and [Anthony Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Kennedy), [Brett Kavanaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brett_Kavanaugh) also for [Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Kennedy), [Amy Coney Barrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett) for [Antonin Scalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonin_Scalia), and [Ketanji Brown Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ketanji_Brown_Jackson) for [Stephen Breyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Breyer). Justices [Gorsuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Gorsuch) and [Kavanaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brett_Kavanaugh) served under [Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Kennedy) during the same term.

[Gorsuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Gorsuch) is the first justice to clerk for and subsequently serve alongside the same justice, serving alongside [Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Kennedy) from April 2017 through Kennedy's retirement in 2018. With the confirmation of Justice Kavanaugh, for the first time a majority of the Supreme Court was composed of former Supreme Court law clerks (Roberts, Breyer, Kagan, Gorsuch and Kavanaugh, now joined by Barrett and Jackson, who replaced Breyer).

Several current Supreme Court justices have also clerked in the federal courts of appeals: John Roberts for Judge [Henry Friendly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Friendly) of the [United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Second_Circuit), Justice [Samuel Alito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Alito) for Judge [Leonard I. Garth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonard_I._Garth) of the [United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Third_Circuit), Elena Kagan for Judge [Abner J. Mikva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abner_J._Mikva) of the [United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit](575245.md), [Neil Gorsuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Gorsuch) for Judge [David B. Sentelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_B._Sentelle) of the [United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_District_of_Columbia), Brett Kavanaugh for Judge [Walter Stapleton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_King_Stapleton) of the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit and Judge [Alex Kozinski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Kozinski) of the [United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Ninth_Circuit), and Amy Coney Barrett for Judge [Laurence Silberman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurence_Silberman) of the [U.S. Court of Appeals for the D.C. Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Court_of_Appeals_for_the_D.C._Circuit).

### Politicization of the court

Clerks hired by each of the justices of the Supreme Court are often given considerable leeway in the opinions they draft. "Supreme Court clerkship appeared to be a nonpartisan institution from the 1940s into the 1980s", according to a study published in 2009 by the law review of [Vanderbilt University Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanderbilt_University_Law_School).[^cite_note-nyt090710-252][^cite_note-253] "As law has moved closer to mere politics, political affiliations have naturally and predictably become proxies for the different political agendas that have been pressed in and through the courts", former federal court of appeals judge [J. Michael Luttig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Michael_Luttig) said.[^cite_note-nyt090710-252]

[David J. Garrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_J._Garrow), professor of history at the [University of Cambridge](25978572.md), stated that the court had thus begun to mirror the political branches of government. "We are getting a composition of the clerk workforce that is getting to be like the House of Representatives", Professor Garrow said. "Each side is putting forward only ideological purists."[^cite_note-nyt090710-252] According to the *Vanderbilt Law Review* study, this politicized hiring trend reinforces the impression that the Supreme Court is "a superlegislature responding to ideological arguments rather than a legal institution responding to concerns grounded in the rule of law."[^cite_note-nyt090710-252]

## Concerns and criticism

Unlike in most high courts, the United States Supreme Court has [lifetime tenure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_tenure), an unusual amount of power over elected branches of government, and a difficult constitution to amend.[^cite_note-254] To these, among other factors, have been attributed by some critics the Court's diminished stature abroad[^cite_note-Liptak-2008-255] and lower approval ratings at home, which have dropped from the mid-60s in the late 1980s to around 40% in the early 2020s. Additional factors cited by critics include the polarization of national politics, ethics scandals, and specific controversial partisan rulings, including the relaxation of [campaign finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaign_finance_in_the_United_States) rules,[^cite_note-Stone-256] increased gerrymandering,[^cite_note-257] weakened voting laws,[^cite_note-258] [*Dobbs v. Jackson*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization) and *[Bush v. Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_v._Gore)*.[^cite_note-\:3-259] The continued consolidation of power by the court and, as a result of its rulings, the Republican Party, has sparked debate over when [democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding) becomes entrenched [single-party rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-party_rule).[^cite_note-\:3-259]

### Approval ratings

Public trust in the court peaked in the late 1980s. Since the 2022 *[Dobbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization)* ruling that overturned *Roe v. Wade* and [devolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution) the regulation of abortion, Democrats and independents have increasingly lost trust in the court, seen the court as political, and expressed support for reforming the institution.[^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261] Historically, the court had relatively more trust than other government institutions.[^cite_note-The_Christian_Science_Monitor_2024_a314-262]

After recording recent high approval ratings in the late 1980s around 66% approval,[^cite_note-263] the court's ratings have declined to an average of around 40% between mid-2021 and February 2024.[^cite_note-264]

### Composition and selection

Further information: [United States Senate § Criticism](24909346.md#Criticism), [United States Electoral College § Impacts and reception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College#Impacts_and_reception), and [Ideological leanings of United States Supreme Court justices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideological_leanings_of_United_States_Supreme_Court_justices)



Composition of the Supreme Court may have flipped if popular vote winners had successful nominations to the court.

The [electoral college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College) (which elects the President who nominates the justices) and the [U.S. Senate](24909346.md) which confirms the justices, have selection biases that favor rural states that tend to vote Republican, resulting in a conservative Supreme Court.[^cite_note-Litt-2020-265] Ziblatt and Levitsky estimate that 3 or 4 of the seats held by conservative justices on the court would be held by justices appointed by a Democratic president if the Presidency and Senate were selected directly by the popular vote.[^cite_note-266] The three Trump appointees to the court were all nominated by a president who finished second in the popular vote and confirmed by Senators representing a minority of Americans.[^cite_note-267] In addition, Clarence Thomas' confirmation in 1991 and Merrick Garland's blocked confirmation in 2016 were both decided by senators representing a minority of Americans.[^cite_note-268] Greg Price also critiqued the Court as [minority rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minoritarianism).[^cite_note-269]

The [Federalist Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalist_Society) acted as a filter for judicial nominations during the first Trump administration,[^cite_note-Federalistsociety-270] ensuring the latest conservative justices lean even further to the right.[^cite_note-Litt-2020-265] 86% of judges Trump appointed to circuit courts and the Supreme Court were Federalist Society members.[^cite_note-271] David Litt critiques it as "an attempt to impose rigid ideological dogma on a profession once known for intellectual freedom."[^cite_note-272] Kate Aronoff criticizes the donations from special interests like fossil fuel companies and other dark money groups to the Federalist Society and related organizations seeking to influence lawyers and Supreme Court Justices.[^cite_note-273]

The 2016 stonewalling of [Merrick Garland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merrick_Garland)'s confirmation and subsequent filling with [Neil Gorsuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Gorsuch) has been critiqued as a 'stolen seat' citing precedent from the 20th century of confirmations during election years,[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275] while proponents cited three blocked nominations between 1844 and 1866.[^cite_note-276] In recent years, Democrats have accused Republican leaders such as [Mitch McConnell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitch_McConnell) of hypocrisy, as they were instrumental in blocking the nomination of Garland, but then quickly confirmed [Amy Coney Barrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett)'s nomination even though both vacancies occurred close to an election.[^cite_note-277]

### Ethics

Supreme Court justices have come under greater scrutiny since 2022,[^cite_note-278] following public disclosures that began with the founder of [Faith and Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faith_and_Action) admissions regarding the organization's long-term influence-peddling scheme, dubbed "Operation Higher Court", designed for wealthy donors among the religious right to gain access to the justices through events held by The [Supreme Court Historical Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_Historical_Society#Controversy).[^cite_note-\:12-279][^cite_note-Marimow-280][^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282]

Ethical controversies have grown during the 2020s, with reports of justices (and their close family members) accepting expensive gifts, travel, business deals, and speaking fees without oversight or [recusals](2245669.md) from cases that present conflicts of interest.[^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284][^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286][^cite_note-287][^cite_note-NYT226-288][^cite_note-289] Spousal income and connections to cases has been redacted from the Justices' ethical disclosure forms[^cite_note-290] while justices, such as [Samuel Alito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Alito#Accusations_of_accepting_gifts) and [Clarence Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Thomas#Nondisclosure_of_finances), failed to disclose many large financial gifts including free vacations valued at as much as \$500,000.[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292]

In 2024, Justices Alito and Thomas refused calls to recuse themselves from [January 6](66307490.md) cases where their spouses have taken public stances or been involved in efforts to overturn the election.[^cite_note-NYT-202405293-293][^cite_note-294][^cite_note-295][^cite_note-\:1-296] In 2017, Neil Gorsuch sold a property he co-owned for \$1.8 million to the CEO of [a prominent law firm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenberg_Traurig),[^cite_note-\:02-297] who was not listed on his ethics form when reporting a profit of between \$250,000 and \$500,000.[^cite_note-\:02-297][^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299]

The criticism intensified after the 2024 *[Trump v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_v._United_States_\(2024\))* decision granted broad immunity to presidents, with Representative [Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria_Ocasio-Cortez) saying she would introduce impeachment articles when Congress is back in session.[^cite_note-300] On July 10, 2024, she filed Articles of Impeachment against Thomas and Alito, citing their "widely documented financial and personal entanglements".[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302][^cite_note-Impeach-118][^cite_note-303] As of late July 2024, nearly 1.4 million people had signed a [moveon.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moveon.org) petition asking Congress to remove Justice Thomas.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305]

President Biden proposed term limits for justices, an enforceable ethics code, and elimination of "immunity for crimes a former president committed while in office".[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308]

Yale professor of constitutional law [Akhil Reed Amar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akhil_Reed_Amar) wrote an op-ed for *[The Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Atlantic)* titled *Something Has Gone Deeply Wrong at the Supreme Court*.[^cite_note-309]

Other criticisms of the Court include weakening [corruption](3608404.md) laws impacting branches beyond the judiciary[^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311] and citing falsehoods in written opinions, often supplied to the justices by amicus briefs from groups advocating a particular outcome.[^cite_note-\:5-220] Allison Orr Larsen, Associate Dean at [William & Mary Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_&_Mary_Law_School), wrote in *[Politico](9172097.md)* that the court should address this by requiring disclosure of all funders of amicus briefs and the studies they cite, only admit briefs that stay within the expertise of the authors (as is required in lower courts), and require the briefs to be submitted much earlier in the process so the history and facts have time to be challenged and uncovered.[^cite_note-\:4-219]

#### Code of Conduct

On November 13, 2023, the court issued its first-ever [Code of Conduct for Justices of the Supreme Court of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code_of_Conduct_for_Justices_of_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States) to set "ethics rules and principles that guide the conduct of the Members of the Court."[^cite_note-Sherman-312][^cite_note-313] The Code has been received by some as a significant first step[^cite_note-314] but does not address the ethics concerns of many notable critics who found the Code was a significantly weakened version of the rules for other federal judges, let alone the legislature and the executive branch, while also lacking an enforcement mechanism.[^cite_note-Sherman-312][^cite_note-315][^cite_note-316] The Code's commentary denied past wrongdoing by saying that the Justices have largely abided by these principles and are simply publishing them now.[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318][^cite_note-Barnes_Marimow_2023_b419-319] This has prompted some criticism that the court hopes to legitimize past and future scandals through this Code.[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321]

The ethics rules guiding the justices are set and enforced by the justices themselves, meaning the members of the court have no external checks on their behavior other than the impeachment of a justice by Congress.[^cite_note-brennancenter.org-2019-322][^cite_note-Marimow-280]

Chief Justice Roberts refused to testify before the Senate Judiciary Committee in April 2023, reasserting his desire for the Supreme Court to continue to monitor itself despite mounting [ethics scandals](#Ethics).[^cite_note-323] Lower courts, by contrast, [discipline](834368.md#Discipline) according to the 1973 Code of Conduct for U.S. judges which is enforced by the Judicial Conduct and Disability Act of 1980.[^cite_note-brennancenter.org-2019-322]

*Article III, Section I*, [Constitution of the United States](31644.md), 1776 establishes that the justices hold their office during good behavior. Thus far only one justice (Associate Justice [Samuel Chase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Chase) in 1804) has ever been impeached, and none has ever been removed from office.[^cite_note-SCchase-117]

The lack of external enforcement of ethics or other conduct violations makes the Supreme Court an outlier in modern organizational best-practices.[^cite_note-brennancenter.org-2019-322] 2024 reform legislation has been blocked by congressional Republicans.[^cite_note-\:1-296]

#### Democratic backsliding

Main article: [Democratic backsliding in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding_in_the_United_States)

Thomas Keck argues that because the Court has historically not served as a strong bulwark for democracy, the Roberts Court had the opportunity to go down in history as a defender of democracy. However, he believes that if the court shields Trump from criminal prosecution (after ensuring his access to the ballot), then the risks that come with an anti-democratic status-quo of the current court will outweigh the dangers that come from court reform (including court packing).[^cite_note-324] [Aziz Z. Huq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aziz_Z._Huq) points to the blocking progress of democratizing institutions, [increasing the disparity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens_United_v._FEC) in wealth and power, and empowering an [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian) [white nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_nationalist) movement as evidence that the Supreme Court has created a "permanent minority" incapable of being defeated democratically.[^cite_note-325]

In a July 2022 research paper entitled "The Supreme Court's Role in the Degradation of U.S. Democracy", the [Campaign Legal Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaign_Legal_Center), founded by Republican [Trevor Potter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trevor_Potter), asserted that the Roberts Court "has turned on our democracy" and was on an "anti-democratic crusade" that had "accelerated and become increasingly extreme with the arrival" of Trump's three appointees.[^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327] A 2024 op-ed by legal reporters [Dahlia Lithwick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dahlia_Lithwick) and [Mark Joseph Stern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Joseph_Stern) expressed a similar view.[^cite_note-328]

### Individual rights

Some of the most notable historical decisions that were criticized for failing to protect individual rights include the *[Dred Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dred_Scott_v._Sandford)* (1857) decision that said people of African descent could not be U.S. citizens or enjoy constitutionally protected rights and privileges,[^cite_note-tws22oct16-329] *[Plessy v. Ferguson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plessy_v._Ferguson)* (1896) that upheld [segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States) under the doctrine of *[separate but equal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separate_but_equal),*[^cite_note-tws23oct04-330] the *[Civil Rights Cases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Cases)* (1883) and *[Slaughter-House Cases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slaughter-House_Cases)* (1873) that all but undermined civil rights legislation enacted during the [Reconstruction era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_era).[^cite_note-331]

However, others argue that the court is too protective of some individual rights, particularly those of people accused of crimes or in detention. For example, Chief Justice [Warren Burger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren_Burger) criticized the [exclusionary rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusionary_rule), and Justice [Scalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonin_Scalia) criticized *[Boumediene v. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boumediene_v._Bush)* for being *too protective* of the rights of [Guantanamo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guantanamo_Bay_Detention_Camp) detainees, arguing [habeas corpus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habeas_corpus) should be limited to sovereign territory.[^cite_note-tws30oct05-332]



Protestors in support of keeping *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)*

After *[Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization)* (2022) overturned *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)* (1973), *[PBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PBS)* reported that the case could start reconsideration of the doctrine of [substantive due process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Substantive_due_process), since a concurrence in the case by Justice [Clarence Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Thomas) argued for that.[^cite_note-PBS_NewsHour-2022-333] Substantive due process has been the main means used by the Supreme Court to [incorporate the Bill of Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incorporation_of_the_Bill_of_Rights) against state and local governments.[^cite_note-334] Justice Thomas has referred to it as '[legal fiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_fiction),'[^cite_note-335] preferring the [Privileges or Immunities Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privileges_or_Immunities_Clause) for incorporation.[^cite_note-336]

### Judicial activism

The Supreme Court has been criticized for engaging in [judicial activism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_activism). This criticism is leveled by those who believe the court should not interpret the law in any way besides through the lens of past precedent or [textualism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textualism). However, those on both sides of the political aisle often level this accusation at the court. The debate around judicial activism typically involves accusing the other side of activism, whilst denying that your own side engages in it.[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338]

Conservatives often cite the decision in *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)* (1973) as an example of liberal judicial activism. In its decision, the court legalized abortion on the basis of a "right to privacy" that they found inherent in the [Due Process Clause](629693.md) of the [Fourteenth Amendment](31666.md).[^cite_note-judicialactivismOxford-339] *Roe v. Wade* was overturned nearly fifty years later by *[Dobbs v. Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson)* (2022), ending the recognition of abortion access as a constitutional right and returning the issue of abortion back to the states. [David Litt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Litt) criticized the decision in *Dobbs* as activism on the part of the court's conservative majority because the court failed to respect past precedent, eschewing the principle of *[stare decisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stare_decisis)* that usually guides the court's decisions.[^cite_note-340]

The decision in *[Brown v. Board of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_v._Board_of_Education)*, which banned racial segregation in public schools was also criticized as activist by conservatives [Pat Buchanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Buchanan),[^cite_note-tws23oct71-341] [Robert Bork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Bork)[^cite_note-342] and [Barry Goldwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Goldwater).[^cite_note-tws22oct33-343] More recently, *[Citizens United v. Federal Election Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens_United_v._Federal_Election_Commission)* was criticized for expanding upon the precedent in *[First National Bank of Boston v. Bellotti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_National_Bank_of_Boston_v._Bellotti)* (1978) that the [First Amendment](31653.md) applies to corporations.[^cite_note-Stone-256]

### Outdated and an outlier

*[Foreign Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Policy)* writer Colm Quinn says that a criticism leveled at the court, as well as other American institutions, is that after two centuries they are beginning to look their age. He cites four features of the United States Supreme Court that make it different from high courts in other countries, and help explain why polarization is an issue in the United States court:[^cite_note-344]

- It is high-profile: the high court in the United States is one of the few courts in the world that can unilaterally strike down legislation passed by other politically accountable branches.

- The United States Constitution [is very difficult to amend](31644.md#Difficult_to_amend): other countries allow for constitutional changes via referendum or with a supermajority in the legislature.

- The United States Supreme Court has a politicized nominating process.

- The United States Supreme Court lacks term limits or mandatory retirements.

[Adam Liptak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Liptak) wrote in 2008 that the court has declined in relevance in other constitutional courts. He cites factors like [American exceptionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_exceptionalism), the relatively few updates to the constitution or the courts, the rightward shift of the court and the diminished stature of the United States abroad.[^cite_note-Liptak-2008-255]

### Power

[Michael Waldman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Waldman) argued that no other country gives its Supreme Court as much power.[^cite_note-345] Warren E. Burger, before becoming Chief Justice, argued that since the Supreme Court has such "unreviewable power", it is likely to "self-indulge itself", and unlikely to "engage in dispassionate analysis".[^cite_note-twsvsfs32-346] [Larry Sabato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Sabato) wrote that the federal courts, and especially the Supreme Court, have excessive power.[^cite_note-tws23oct16-105] Suja A. Thomas argues the Supreme Court has taken most of the constitutionally defined power from [juries in the United States](27182151.md) for itself[^cite_note-\:022-347] thanks in part to the influence of legal elites and companies that prefer judges over juries[^cite_note-348] as well as the inability of the jury to defend its power.[^cite_note-349]

Some members of Congress considered the results from the 2021–2022 term a shift of government power into the Supreme Court, and a "judicial coup".[^cite_note-350] The 2021–2022 term of the court was the first full term following the appointment of three judges by Republican president [Donald Trump](4848272.md) — [Neil Gorsuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Gorsuch), [Brett Kavanaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brett_Kavanaugh), and [Amy Coney Barrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett) — which created a six-strong conservative majority on the court. Subsequently, at the end of the term, the court issued a number of decisions that favored this conservative majority while significantly changing the landscape with respect to rights. These included *[Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization)* which overturned *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)* and *[Planned Parenthood v. Casey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_Parenthood_v._Casey)* in recognizing abortion is not a constitutional right, *[New York State Rifle & Pistol Association, Inc. v. Bruen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_State_Rifle_&_Pistol_Association,_Inc._v._Bruen)* which made public possession of guns a protected right under the Second Amendment, *[Carson v. Makin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carson_v._Makin)* and *[Kennedy v. Bremerton School District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennedy_v._Bremerton_School_District)* which both weakened the [Establishment Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Establishment_Clause) separating church and state, and *[West Virginia v. EPA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Virginia_v._EPA)* which weakened the power of executive branch agencies to interpret their congressional mandate.[^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352][^cite_note-353]

#### Federalism debate

There has been debate throughout American history about the boundary between federal and state power. While Framers such as [James Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Madison)[^cite_note-tws24oct22-354] and [Alexander Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton)[^cite_note-tws24oct47-355] argued in *[The Federalist Papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Federalist_Papers)* that their then-proposed Constitution would not infringe on the power of state governments,[^cite_note-tws27oct501-356][^cite_note-tws27oct502-357][^cite_note-tws27oct503-358][^cite_note-tws27oct504-359] others argue that expansive [federal power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_power) is good and consistent with the Framers' wishes.[^cite_note-tws24oct10-360] The [Tenth Amendment to the United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution) explicitly states that "powers not delegated to the United States by the Constitution, nor prohibited by it to the States, are reserved to the States respectively, or to the people."

The court has been criticized for giving the [federal government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Government_of_the_United_States) too much power to interfere with state authority. One criticism is that it has allowed the federal government to misuse the [Commerce Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commerce_Clause) by upholding regulations and legislation which have little to do with interstate commerce, but that were enacted under the guise of regulating interstate commerce; and by voiding state legislation for allegedly interfering with interstate commerce. For example, the Commerce Clause was used by the Fifth Circuit Court of Appeals to uphold the Endangered Species Act, thus protecting six endemic species of insect near Austin, Texas, despite the fact that the insects had no commercial value and did not travel across state lines; the Supreme Court let that ruling stand without comment in 2005.[^cite_note-tws30oct08-361] Chief Justice [John Marshall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Marshall) asserted Congress's power over interstate commerce was "complete in itself, may be exercised to its utmost extent, and acknowledges no limitations, other than are prescribed in the Constitution."[^cite_note-tws27oct505-362] Justice Alito said congressional authority under the Commerce Clause is "quite broad";[^cite_note-tws30oct09-363] commentator [Robert B. Reich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_B._Reich) suggests debate over the Commerce Clause continues today.[^cite_note-tws27oct505-362]

Advocates of [states' rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States'_rights), such as constitutional scholar [Kevin Gutzman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Gutzman), have also criticized the court, saying it has misused the Fourteenth Amendment to undermine state authority. Justice [Brandeis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Brandeis), in arguing for allowing the states to operate without federal interference, suggested that states should be [laboratories of democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laboratories_of_democracy).[^cite_note-tws30oct03-364] One critic wrote "the great majority of Supreme Court rulings of unconstitutionality involve state, not federal, law."[^cite_note-tws23oct15-365] Others see the Fourteenth Amendment as a positive force that extends "protection of those rights and guarantees to the state level."[^cite_note-tws30oct02-366]

More recently, in *[Gamble v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamble_v._United_States)*, the Court examined the doctrine of "[separate sovereigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separate_sovereigns)", whereby a criminal defendant can be prosecuted in state court as well as federal court on separate charges for the same offense.[^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368]

### Ruling on political questions

Some Court decisions have been criticized for injecting the court into the political arena, and deciding questions that are the purview of the elected branches of government. The *Bush v. Gore* decision, in which the Supreme Court intervened in the 2000 presidential election, awarding [George W. Bush](3414021.md) the presidency over [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore), received scrutiny as political based on the controversial justifications used by the five conservative justices to elevate a fellow conservative to the presidency.[^cite_note-tws23oct12-369][^cite_note-tws23oct04-330][^cite_note-tws22oct40-370][^cite_note-tws23oct01-371][^cite_note-tws23oct08-372] The ruling was also controversial in applying logic only for that race, as opposed to drawing on or creating consistent precedent.[^cite_note-373]

### Secretive proceedings

Further information: [Shadow docket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_docket) and [List of United States Supreme Court leaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_Supreme_Court_leaks)

The court has been criticized for keeping its deliberations hidden from public view.[^cite_note-374][^cite_note-375] For example, the increasing use of a '[shadow docket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_docket)' facilitates the court making decisions in secret without knowing how each Justice came to their decision.[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377] In 2024, after comparing the analysis of shadow-docket decisions to [Kremlinology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kremlinology), Matt Ford called this trend of secrecy "increasingly troubling", arguing the court's power comes entirely from persuasion and explanation.[^cite_note-378]

A 2007 review of [Jeffrey Toobin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Toobin)'s book compared the Court to a cartel where its inner-workings are mostly unknown, arguing this lack of transparency reduces scrutiny which hurts ordinary Americans who know little about the nine extremely consequential Justices.[^cite_note-tws23oct12-369] A 2010 poll found that 61% of American voters agreed that [televising Court hearings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameras_in_the_Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States) would "be good for democracy", and 50% of voters stated they would watch Court proceedings if they were televised.[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380]

### Too few cases

[Ian Millhiser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Millhiser) of [Vox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vox_Media) speculates that the decades-long decline in cases heard could be due to the increasing political makeup of judges, that he says might be more interested in settling political disputes than legal ones.[^cite_note-381]

### Too slow

British constitutional scholar [Adam Tomkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Tomkins) sees flaws in the American system of having courts (and specifically the Supreme Court) act as checks on the Executive and Legislative branches; he argues that because the courts must wait, sometimes for years, for cases to navigate their way through the system, their ability to restrain other branches is severely weakened.[^cite_note-tws23oct77-382][^cite_note-tws2nov02-383] In contrast, various other countries have a dedicated [constitutional court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_court) that has original jurisdiction on constitutional claims brought by persons or political institutions; for example, the [Federal Constitutional Court of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Constitutional_Court_of_Germany), which can declare a law unconstitutional when challenged.

Critics have accused the Court of "slow-walking" important cases relating to then-former president Donald Trump in order to benefit his election chances in the face of the [2024 United States presidential election](59568546.md).[^cite_note-\:2-384] The Court is considering a [presidential immunity claim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_prosecution_of_Donald_Trump_\(election_obstruction_case\)#Immunity_dispute) as part of the [Federal prosecution of Donald Trump (election obstruction case)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_prosecution_of_Donald_Trump_\(election_obstruction_case\)). Critics argue that the Court has acted slowly in order to delay this case until after the election. They point out that the Court can move quickly when it wants to, as it did when it disregarded typical procedures in *[Bush v. Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_v._Gore)*, granting the petition on a Saturday, receiving briefs on Sunday, holding oral arguments on Monday, and issuing the final opinion on Tuesday.[^cite_note-\:2-384] Author Sonja West, of [*Slate*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slate_\(magazine\)), argues that the Federal prosecution of Donald Trump (election obstruction case) is of similar importance to *Bush v. Gore* and should therefore be treated as expeditiously, but the Court seems to be taking the opposite approach.[^cite_note-\:2-384]

### Leaks and inadvertent publications

Sometimes draft opinions are deliberately [leaked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/News_leak) or inadvertently released before they are published. Such [releases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_Supreme_Court_leaks) are often purported to harm the court's reputation.[^cite_note-385] Chief Justice Roberts has previously described leaks as an "egregious breach of trust" that "undermine the integrity of our operations" in reference to the leaked draft opinion for *[Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization)*.[^cite_note-386]

In addition to leaks, the Court has sometimes mistakenly released opinions before they are ready to be published. On June 26, 2024, the Court inadvertently posted an opinion for *[Moyle v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moyle_v._United_States)* to its website that seemed to indicate that the court would temporarily allow abortions in medical emergencies in Idaho.[^cite_note-387] The official opinion was posted the next day, which returned the case to the lower courts without a ruling on the merits.

- *[Chevron v. N.R.D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chevron_U.S.A.,_Inc._v._Natural_Resources_Defense_Council,_Inc.)* (1984, [Chevron deference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chevron_deference))

- *[Bush v. Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bush_v._Gore)* (2000, presidential election)

- *[Lawrence v. Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_v._Texas)* (2003, sodomy)

- *[District of Columbia v. Heller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_v._Heller)* (2008, gun rights)

- *[Citizens United v. FEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens_United_v._FEC)* (2010, campaign finance)

- *[United States v. Windsor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Windsor)* (2013, same-sex marriage)

- *[Shelby County v. Holder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shelby_County_v._Holder)* (2013, voting rights)

- *[Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges)* (2015, same-sex marriage)

- *[Bostock v. Clayton County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bostock_v._Clayton_County)* (2020, discrimination on [LGBT workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_employment_discrimination_in_the_United_States))

- *[McGirt v. Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McGirt_v._Oklahoma)* (2020, [tribal reservation rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribal_sovereignty_in_the_United_States))

- *[Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization)* (2022, abortion)

- *[New York State Rifle and Pistol Association v. Bruen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_State_Rifle_and_Pistol_Association_v._Bruen)* (2022, firearms)

- *[Students for Fair Admissions v. Harvard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Students_for_Fair_Admissions_v._Harvard)* (2023, [affirmative action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action_in_the_United_States))

- *[Loper Bright Enterprises v. Raimondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loper_Bright_Enterprises_v._Raimondo)* (2024, overruled [Chevron deference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chevron_deference))

- [*Trump v. United States* (2024)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_v._United_States_\(2024\)) ([presidential immunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_immunity))

The Court continued, "In our view, however, the pro forma sessions count as sessions, not as periods of recess. We hold that, for purposes of the Recess Appointments Clause, the Senate is in session when it says it is, provided that, under its own rules, it retains the capacity to transact Senate business. The Senate met that standard here." Later, the opinion states: "For these reasons, we conclude that we must give great weight to the Senate's own determination of when it is and when it is not in session. But our deference to the Senate cannot be absolute. When the Senate is without the capacity to act, under its own rules, it is not in session even if it so declares."

Had \[O'Connor] anticipated that the chief justice would not serve out the next Supreme Court term, she told me after his death, she would have delayed her own retirement for a year rather than burden the court with two simultaneous vacancies. \[...] Her reason for leaving was that her husband, suffering from Alzheimer's disease, needed her care at home.

[^cite_note-171]: &#x20;Ward, Artemus (2003). [*Deciding to Leave: The Politics of Retirement from the United States Supreme Court*](http://www.sunypress.edu/pdf/60710.pdf) (PDF). SUNY Press. p. 9. One byproduct of the increased \[retirement benefit] provisions \[in 1954], however has been a dramatic rise in the number of justices engaging in succession politics by trying to time their departures to coincide with a compatible president. The most recent departures have been partisan, some more blatantly than others, and have bolstered arguments to reform the process. A second byproduct has been an increase in justices staying on the Court past their ability to adequately contribute.

On May 28, \[U.S. Attorney General William] Moody did something unprecedented, then and now. He filed a petition charging Sheriff Shipp, six deputies and 19 leaders of the lynch mob with contempt of the Supreme Court. The justices unanimously approved the petition and agreed to retain original jurisdiction in the matter. ... May 24, 1909, stands out in the annals of the U.S. Supreme Court. On that day, the court announced a verdict after holding the first and only criminal trial in its history.

[^cite_note-203]: &#x20;Glick, Joshua (April 2003). ["On the road: The Supreme Court and the history of circuit riding"](https://web.archive.org/web/20180925025629/http://supremecourthistory.org/assets/schs_publications-circuitriding.pdf) (PDF). *Cardozo Law Review*. **24**. Archived from [the original](http://www.supremecourthistory.org/assets/schs_publications-circuitriding.pdf) (PDF) on September 25, 2018. Retrieved September 24, 2018. Gradually, however, circuit riding lost support. The Court's increasing business in the nation's capital following the Civil War made the circuit riding seem anachronistic and impractical and a slow shift away from the practice began. The Judiciary Act of 1869 established a separate circuit court judiciary. The justices retained nominal circuit riding duties until 1891 when the Circuit Court of Appeals Act was passed. With the Judicial Code of 1911, Congress officially ended the practice. The struggle between the legislative and judicial branches over circuit riding was finally concluded.
